Inherited retinal diseases (IRD) are a leading cause of blindness in the working 
age population and in children. The scope of this review is to familiarise 
clinicians and scientists with the current landscape of molecular genetics, 
clinical phenotype, retinal imaging and therapeutic prospects/completed trials 
in IRD. Herein we present in a comprehensive and concise manner: (i) macular 
dystrophies (Stargardt disease (ABCA4), X-linked retinoschisis (RS1), Best 
disease (BEST1), PRPH2-associated pattern dystrophy, Sorsby fundus dystrophy 
(TIMP3), and autosomal dominant drusen (EFEMP1)), (ii) cone and cone-rod 
dystrophies (GUCA1A, PRPH2, ABCA4, KCNV2 and RPGR), (iii) predominant rod or 
rod-cone dystrophies (retinitis pigmentosa, enhanced S-Cone syndrome (NR2E3), 
Bietti crystalline corneoretinal dystrophy (CYP4V2)), (iv) Leber congenital 
amaurosis/early-onset severe retinal dystrophy (GUCY2D, CEP290, CRB1, RDH12, 
RPE65, TULP1, AIPL1 and NMNAT1), (v) cone dysfunction syndromes (achromatopsia 
(CNGA3, CNGB3, PDE6C, PDE6H, GNAT2, ATF6), X-linked cone dysfunction with myopia 
and dichromacy (Bornholm Eye disease; OPN1LW/OPN1MW array), oligocone 
trichromacy, and blue-cone monochromatism (OPN1LW/OPN1MW array)). Whilst we use 
the aforementioned classical phenotypic groupings, a key feature of IRD is that 
it is characterised by tremendous heterogeneity and variable expressivity, with 
several of the above genes associated with a range of phenotypes.

ABSTRACT 
Inherited retinal diseases (IRD) are a leading cause of blindness in the working age 
population and children. The scope of this review is to familiarise clinicians and 
scientists with the current landscape of molecular genetics, clinical phenotype, retinal 
imaging and therapeutic prospects/completed trials in IRD. Herein we present in a 
comprehensive and concise manner: (i) macular dystrophies (Stargardt disease 
(ABCA4), X -linked retinoschisis ( RS1), Best disease ( BEST1), PRPH2-associated 
pattern dystrophy, Sorsby fundus dystrophy (TIMP3), and autosomal dominant drusen 
(EFEMP1)), (ii) cone and cone -rod dystrophies (GUCA1A, PRPH2, ABCA4, KCNV2 
and RPGR), (iii) predominant rod or rod-cone dystrophies (retinitis pigmentosa, 
enhanced S -Cone syndrome ( NR2E3), Bietti crystalline corneoretinal dystrophy 
(CYP4V2)), (iv) Leber congenital amaurosis/ early-onset severe retinal dystrophy 
(GUCY2D, CEP290, CRB1, RDH12, RPE65, TULP1, AIPL1 and NMNAT1), (v) cone 
dysfunction syndromes (achromatopsia (CNGA3, CNGB3, PDE6C, PDE6H, GNAT2, 
ATF6), X -linked cone dysfunction with myopia and dichromacy (Bornholm Eye 
disease; OPN1LW/OPN1MW array), oligocone trichromacy, and blue -cone 
monochromatism ( OPN1LW/OPN1MW array). Whilst we use the aforementioned 
classical phe notypic groupings, the beauty of IRD is that it is characterised by 
unrivalled heterogeneity and variable expressivity, with several of the above 
genotypes associated with a range of phenotypes. 
Journal Pre-proof

 
1. INTRODUCTION 1 
The inherited retinal diseases (IRD) have diverse clinical presentations, which can result in 2 
a diagnostic challenge . Diagnosis commonly involves multimodal retinal imaging, 3 
psychophysical and electrophysiological evaluation, and genetic testing. They constitute the 4 
leading cause of legal blindness in England and Wales amongst working -age adults, and 5 
the second commonest in childhood, (Liew et al., 2014)  having a great impact on the 6 
individuals and families affected, (Galvin et al., 2020)  and they are subject to a range of 7 
interventions and research avenues.(Smith et al., 2015, Georgiou et al., 2021c)  Here, we 8 
categorise IRD on the basis of the primarily affected retinal cell -type and disease natural 9 
history (stationary or progressive). In the current review, w e prioritised t he selected 10 
conditions based on their prevalence, distinct clinical and phenotypic features and research 11 
advancements. Other recent reviews have explored in great depth more specific topics such 12 
as ABCA4-associated retinopathy, (Cremers et al., 2020)  and non-syndromic retinitis 13 
pigmentosa (Verbakel et al., 2018), or other resources provide extensive lists of genes and 14 
loci causing IRD ( e.g. RetNet - Retinal Information Network (uth.edu)) . Herein, w e 15 
summarised a great body of work performed in genotyping and phenotyping IRD at 16 
Moorfields Eye Hospital over the last two decades. Rod dysfunction syndromes including all 17 
forms of congenital stationary night blindness, chorioretinal dystrophies, syndromic forms of 18 
retinal degeneration, and developmental macular disorders  were excluded in the current 19 
review (Michaelides et al., 20 12, Georgiou et al., 2020a, Zeitz et al., 2015) . We aim to 20 
present the recent progress in understanding the molecular basis, natural history, clinical 21 
features, and prospects of treatment for  macular dystrophies (MD), cone and cone-rod 22 
dystrophies (COD/CORD), rod-cone dystrophies (RCD), Leber congenital amaurosis (LCA), 23 
and cone dysfunction syndromes (CDS). 24 
  25

2. MACULAR DYSTROPHIES 26 
Macular dystrophies (MD) are a group of IRD characterised by bilateral, relatively 27 
symmetrical macular abnormalities that significantly impair central visual function.(Rahman 28 
et al., 2019)  Whilst these dystrophies often have imaging/fundoscopic abnormalities 29 
primarily confined to the macula, the majority in fact have psychophysical, 30 
electrophysiological or histopathological evidence of generalised retinal involvement. Below 31 
we present some of the commonest subtypes,(Pontikos et al., 2020)  including Stargardt 32 
disease (STGD1), Best disease (BD), X-linked retinoschisis (XLRS), pattern dystrophy (PD), 33 
Sorsby fundus dystrophy (SFD), and autosomal dominant drusen (ADD). 34 
 35 
2.1. Stargardt disease 36 
STGD1 is the most common monogenic MD, classically associated with central macular 37 
atrophy and deposition of lipofuscin (bisretinoids) in the retinal pigment epithelium (RPE), 38 
leading to the classical fundus appearance of retinal flecks  (Figure 1A). The spectrum of 39 
disease is highly variable, ranging from isolated macular disease to generalised cone and 40 
rod system involvement  (Khan et al., 2018c, Tanna et al., 2019, Fujinami et al., 2015a, 41 
Fujinami et al., 2013d, Tanna et al., 2018, Gill et al., 2019). The severity of disease and rate 42 
of progression varies widely between individuals, and largely relate s to age of onset and 43 
allelic heterogeneity (see bel ow); namely childhood -onset STGD 1, versus, adult -onset 44 
STGD1 versus, late-onset ('foveal sparing') STGD1.  45 
 46 
2.1.1. Molecular Genetics 47 
ABCA4 (STGD1, OMIM #248200) is part of the ATP-binding cassette (ABC) family that are 48 
involved in the active transport of various substrates across cellular membranes. The 49 
pathophysiology of STGD1 is due to defective ABCA4 transport of retinoids (as part of the 50 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
2 
visual cycle), resulting in an abnormal accumulation of lipofuscin and related toxic by -51 
products (including A2E) in the RPE and photoreceptors, with subsequent cell dysfunction 52 
and death overtime. (Dean, 2005)  While ABCA4-associated STGD 1, is an autosomal 53 
recessive condition, there are three autosomal dominant MD that have phenotypic features 54 
that can overlap with some of the presentations of STGD1 (including a bull's -eye 55 
maculopathy). The first is due to disease -causing variants in ELOVL4 (STGD3, OMIM 56 
#600110), the second is more common and due to variants in PROM1 (STGD4, OMIM 57 
#603786) and the third are cases of pattern dystrophy due to PRPH2 variants. ABCA4-58 
associated STGD1 stands out from other genetic conditions, due to a large number of deep-59 
intronic variants found to affect RNA splicing  (Bauwens et al., 2015, Sangermano et al., 60 
2019, Bax et al., 2015, Braun et al., 2013). 61 
 62 
2.1.2. Genotype-Phenotype Correlations 63 
There is increasing evidence that onset relates to the severity of the underlying ABCA4 64 
variants, with childhood -onset STGD 1 being associated with more deleterious variants 65 
(including bi-allelic nonsense variants) compared to adult -onset or the later onset foveal -66 
sparing STGD 1 (more frequently involving at least one missense variant).  (Fakin et al., 67 
2016a, Fakin et al., 2016b, Georgiou et al., 2019a, Cremers et al., 2020, Maugeri et al., 68 
1999) The degree of ABCA4 inactivation also relates to the ABCA4 disease spectrum, with 69 
STGD1 associated with milder sequence variants and thereby often milder inactivation of 70 
ABCA4 and a milder phenotype  (including isolated macular dysfunction) , compared to 71 
severe variants, resulting in complete absence of ABCA4 function, and thereby more severe 72 
disease (including generalised retinal involvement and more profound loss of central visual 73 
function). The vast allelic heterogeneity (more than 2000 disease-causing ABCA4 variants) 74 
makes genotype-phenotype correlations challenging (Burke and Tsang, 2011), although the 75 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
3 
impact of some alleles and allelic combinations ha ve been ascertained in investigations of 76 
large cohorts (Fakin et al., 2016a, Fakin et al., 2016b, Georgiou et al., 2019a, Maugeri et 77 
al., 1999), including a recent machine learning study (Glinton et al., 2022). 78 
 79 
2.1.3. Clinical Assessment 80 
The most common presenting phenotype of STGD1 is of retinal flecks usually surrounding 81 
a central area of maculopathy , or macular atrophy, with or without variable mid-peripheral 82 
distribution, most clearly visualised with fundus autofluorescence (FAF) imaging  (Figure 83 
1B). Over time, macular atrophy develops, causing increasing visual impairment with 84 
disease progression. Patients typically present with reduced central visual function, with 85 
highly variable visual acuity (VA) depending on the degree of foveal involvement. (Fujinami 86 
et al., 2013d) Colour vision abnormalities, photophobia and slow dark adaptation are also 87 
common clinical presentations. Onset is most commonly in childhood, with the next most 88 
prevalent age group being early adulthood, and least frequently in later adulthood (late-onset 89 
/ foveal-sparing STGD1).(Fujinami et al., 2013c, Khan et al., 2018c, Tanna et al., 2019, 90 
Fujinami et al., 2015a, Fujinami et al., 2013d)  The severity of visual impairment is also 91 
dependent on the age of disease onset i.e. early -onset STGD1 is associated with more 92 
severely compromised vision and po orer outcomes.(Lambertus et al., 2015, Tanna et al., 93 
2018, Fujinami et al., 2015a, Georgiou et al., 2019a)  There is a slow progressive loss of 94 
retinal structure and function over time, however there is marked variability in maculopathy 95 
both within and between families, suggesting that other important factors influence 96 
phenotype, including genetic modifiers and the environment (Fujinami et al., 2015a, Fujinami 97 
et al., 2013d, Tanna et al., 2019).  98 
 99

2.1.4. Electrophysiology 100 
The electrophysiological phenotype is variable and has been categorised into 3 groups 101 
based on the full-field ERG (ffERG), with almost all patients having abnormal pattern ERG 102 
(pERG) P50 component, indicative of macular dysfunction  (Lois et al., 2001) ; Group 1 is 103 
characterised by normal ffERG - in keeping with isolated macular dysfunction; patients in 104 
Group 2 have abnormal cone-mediated ERGs and normal rod-mediated ERG components 105 
- indicative of generalised cone system dysfunction; and in Group 3, patients have both 106 
abnormal cone - and rod -mediated ERGs - indicating generalised cone and rod system 107 
dysfunction. These ERG groups have been shown to have prognostic value in STGD1; with 108 
Group 1 being associated with a more favourable prognosis and an 80% likelihood of not 109 
developing generalised retinal dysfunction  over a 10 -year period . Groups 2 and  3 are 110 
associated with the worst visual prognosis and progression, resulting in worse VA and 111 
additional peripheral visual loss (Fujinami et al., 2013a, Lois et al., 2004). 112 
 113 
2.1.5. Imaging 114 
Fundus fluorescein angiography (FFA) demonstrates a dark choroidal phase due to masking 115 
from lipofuscin deposition in the RPE , but this technique has largely been superseded by  116 
optical coherence tomography (OCT)  and fundus autofluorescence (FAF) in the diagnosis 117 
of STGD1. A characteristic pattern of flecks of increased signal on FAF imaging is typically 118 
seen in the early stages, which may evolve into low density foci that expand into confluent 119 
areas of macular atrophy in some cases. Importantly, up to a third of children will not have 120 
flecks on imaging/examination at presentation, with these developing over subsequent 121 
years - another reason the diagnosis can be delayed (Fujinami et al., 2015a). FAF imaging 122 
may serve as a monitoring tool , and decreased autofluorescence area measurements can 123 
be used as a structural outcome for interventional clinical trials. (Strauss et al., 2019, 124 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
5 
Georgiou et al., 2019a)  The peripheral retinal FAF findings and the classification of the 125 
posterior pole appearance can be achieved with ultra-wide field FAF (UWF -FAF).(Strauss 126 
et al., 2017b, Strauss et al., 2017a, Chen et al., 2019, Klufas et al., 2018) UWF-FAF pattern 127 
and extent of retinal changes can have prognostic implication for the extent of subsequent 128 
retinal degeneration and may be a suitable alter native to the aforementioned ERG-based 129 
prognostication; although caution should be exercised in using UWF -FAF exclusively for 130 
childhood-onset STGD1 predictions of future progression.(Daich Varela et al., 2023d)  131 
 OCT sensitively quantifies RPE atrophy and the severity and extent of outer  retinal 132 
loss (photoreceptor l oss).(Tanna et al., 2019)  Moreover, it can identify  asymptomatic 133 
childhood-onset STGD1, by demonstrating hyperreflectivity at the base of the foveal outer 134 
nuclear layer. (Khan et al., 2018c)  The anatomical level of the retinal flecks can be 135 
demonstrated and correlated with visual function.(Cai et al., 2018, Tanna et al., 2018, Melillo 136 
et al., 2016, Greenstein et al., 2017, Ritter et al., 2013, Fujinami et al., 2013d, Ergun et al., 137 
2005) The earliest structural changes of the disease are juxtafoveal and appear to spare the 138 
foveola (Figure 1C).(Khan et al., 2018c)  139 
 Despite the RPE dogma in STGD1, we believe photoreceptor loss is likely to precede 140 
RPE degeneration; both of which contribute to choriocapillaris loss. (Georgiou et al., 2018, 141 
Strauss et al., 2016a, Alabduljalil et al., 2019)  In vivo cellular imaging using adaptive optics 142 
(AO), has demonstrated reduced cone densities and increased photoreceptor spacing  143 
(Figure 1D).(Song et al., 2015, Song et al., 2019, Michaelides et al., 2014)  The cones can 144 
be reliably counted, and tracked over time.(Tanna et al., 2017a) 145 
 146 
2.1.6. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 147 
Light avoidance using tinted spe ctacles may confer benefit in ABCA4-associated 148 
retinopathy by inhibiting A2E production,(Mata et al., 2000) which produces DNA-damaging 149 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
6 
epoxides.(Sparrow et al., 2003b)  vitamin A should also be avoided in ABCA4-associated 150 
retinopathy as it may enhance A2E production and, therefore, disease progression.(Sparrow 151 
et al., 2003a) A study investigated the effect of long -term protection from light exposure in 152 
disease progression, using FAF imaging and an occlusive contact lens for >12 months. 153 
Occluded eyes showed reduced progression, with the study being limited by the small 154 
number of patients (Teussink et al., 2015). 155 
 Pharmacotherapy directly or indirectly targeting the visual cycle has been developed, 156 
including the complement -mediated response to accumulated by -products of the visual 157 
cycle.(Lu et al., 2017) Drugs such as soraprazan, emixustat, ALK-001, LBS-008, STG-001, 158 
fenretinide and A1120, target the visual cycle by impeding formation (or enhance removal 159 
(soraprazan)) of A2E and lipofuscin, by either slowing the rate of vitamin A dimeri sation 160 
(ALK-001), or by competitive inhibitory mechanisms on the retinal binding protein -4 (LBS-161 
008, STG-001, fenretinide and A1120), or by modulating the activity of RPE65 (emixustat). 162 
Many of these drugs are in phase I/II or III trials (LBS -008: NCT03735810, emixustat: 163 
NCT03772665 and  NCT03033108, ALK -001: NCT02402660). Avacincaptad pegol, a 164 
complement C5 inhibitor, is also being investigated in a phase II trial (NCT03364153), as is 165 
an antioxidant supplementation (saffron) (NCT01278277). 166 
 Pre-clinical studies in gene replacement that showed phenotypic improvement in 167 
ABCA4-/- mice have encouraged the development of human gene therapy trials; (Allocca et 168 
al., 2008, Han et al., 2012)  currently employing a lentiviral vector (NCT01736592, 169 
NCT01367444).(Parker et al., 2016)  Adeno-associated virus (AAV) has many adv antages 170 
over lentiviral vectors but has limited cargo capacity; several strategies are being explored 171 
to accommodate the large ABCA4 gene, including dual vector approaches,  and thereby 172 
commence AAV-based gene therapy trials. (Allocca et al., 2008, Parker et al., 2016) 173 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
7 
 In advanced disease, cell replacement strategies and optogenetics offer potential 174 
benefit. The only phase I/II clinical trial (NCT01469832) of human embryonic stem cell 175 
(hESC) derived RPE cells in STGD1 has been completed.(Schwartz et al., 2015) (Mehat et 176 
al., 2018 ) Findings from the UK site of this trial identified subretinal hyperpigmentation 177 
consistent with the survival of viable transplanted hESC -derived RPE cells. Borderline 178 
improvements in VA were noted in 4 of 12 patients; however, microperimetry did not 179 
demonstrate evidence of functional benefit at 12 months. Further trials are anticipated, 180 
including evaluation of combined RPE and photoreceptor transplants, which are either 181 
derived from hESCs or induced pluripotent stem cells (iPSC).  182 
Multi-characteristic opsin (MCO) transgene is an ambient light activatable 183 
optogenetic monotherapy delivered with an AAV2 vector via intravitreal injection. MCO-010 184 
(and other optogenetics approaches)  has the potential to restore vision irrespective of the 185 
underlying gene mutation, because it is directed at bipolar cells, and intact photoreceptors 186 
are not required. A phase II clinical trial investigating MCO -010 for STGD1  (STARLIGHT, 187 
NCT05417126) has been completed. Subjects (n=6) receiving MCO -010, demonstrated 188 
clinically meaning ful improvements in BCVA and an approximate 3 dB gain in mean 189 
sensitivity, as measured by Octopus visual field perimetry per investigators (ASRS 2023). 190 
Further peer-reviewed results are anticipated.  191 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
8 
2.2. Best disease (BD) 192 
Autosomal dominant vitelliform ma cular dystrophy (BD; BEST1, OMIM 607854) is the 193 
second most common MD, due to disease -causing variants in BEST1.(Petrukhin et al., 194 
1998). Although BD is usually considered a macula r dystrophy, it is associated with 195 
generalised (retina-wide) RPE dysfunction.   196 
 197 
2.2.1. Molecular Genetics 198 
BEST1 disease-causing variants  underlying BD are most commonly missense variants . 199 
Most of the variants identified occur in the first half of the gene. Haploinsufficiency is 200 
tolerated, and not associated with BD phenotype. Specific variants are reported to cause a 201 
less severe phenotype than others ( e.g. p.(Ala243Val)) (Khan et al., 2018b) . In autosomal 202 
recessive bestrophinopathy (ARB), compound heterozygous null variants in BEST1 typically 203 
result in more widespread posterior pole changes, with development of a progressive retinal 204 
dystrophy from late childhood or early adolescence (Casalino et al., 2020). Both disorders 205 
are typically characterised by generalised RPE dysfunction, evident as a reduced light peak 206 
to dark trough ratio on electro-oculogram ( EOG) testing (see below) , more c onsistently 207 
severe in ARB than in autosomal dominant B D; with ffERGs also being abnormal  in ARB 208 
(Borman et al., 2011, Khan et al., 2018b).  209 
 BEST1 encodes bestrophin-1, a calcium-sensitive chloride channel localised to the 210 
basolateral membrane of RPE cells, that regulate the ionic environment in the RPE and/or 211 
subretinal space. Dysregulation of this function  results in the vitelliform deposition seen in 212 
BD, in part due to an alteration in the adhesiveness of the interphotoreceptor matrix to the 213 
RPE.(Park et al., 2009, Marmorstein et al., 2009, Yu et al., 2006, Sun et al., 2002) Variants 214 
in other genes can also result in a similar vitelliform phenotype as seen in BD, includ ing 215 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
9 
PRPH2, IMPG1,(Meunier et al., 2014, Manes et al., 2013) and IMPG2.(Meunier et al., 2014, 216 
Georgiou et al., 2022a)  217 
 218 
2.2.2. Clinical Assessment 219 
The onset of BD is generally in early childhood up to late teenage years. (MacDonald and 220 
Lee, 1993) BD is often associated with hypermetropia which needs correction in childhood 221 
to reduce the likelihood of amblyopia; ARB is typically associated with a greater degree of 222 
hypermetropia and a high risk of angle -closure glaucoma, thereby often necessitating 223 
prophylactic intervention to prevent acute angle-closure.(Casalino et al., 2020)  224 
 Stage 1 is characterised by a normal fundus or minimal RPE changes (pre-vitelliform, 225 
Figure 2). The classical appearance of BD is the single, bilateral symmetrical egg-yolk-like 226 
(vitelliform) lesion at the fovea (Stage 2, Figure 2). Over time, this lesion can start to undergo 227 
resorption, progressing to a 'pseudohypopyon' appearance, with the subretinal yellow 228 
material gravitating inferior ly within the lesion (Stage 3 , Figure 2 ). Stage s 1 and 2 are 229 
associated with normal VA, and patients can be identified co -incidentally or during a family 230 
survey; with VA reduction usually starting from Stage 3 onwards. Further progression can 231 
result in a 'vitelliruptive stage', due to further breakdown of subretinal material (Stage 4 , 232 
Figure 2). End-stage disease (Stage 5 , Figure 2) is characterised by either atrophy , sub-233 
RPE fibrosis, or choroidal neovascularisation (CNV). Even though fundus features can be 234 
classified into different stages, there is rarely predictable progression from one to the other; 235 
with the prognosis often being relatively good in many patients, despite marked intrafamilial 236 
variability.(Schachat et al., 2018)  Unlike dominant disease, ARB is often characterised by 237 
diffuse RPE irregularity of the posterior pole, multifocal vitelliform deposits  and patchy 238 
atrophy (Figure 2B), often associated with subretinal fluid ( SRF) and/or cystoid macular 239 
oedema (CMO)(Casalino et al., 2020, Boon et al., 2013) . An atypical presentation of AD 240 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
10 
BEST1- disease is unilateral BD ( Figure 2C), with otherwise clinical appearance typical of 241 
and consistent with BD at various stages (Arora et al., 2016). AD BEST1- disease can also 242 
be multifocal (Boon et al., 2007). 243 
 244 
2.2.3. Electrophysiology 245 
The EOG typically demonstrates a subnormal light peak to dark trough ratio, in keeping with 246 
marked generalised RPE dysfunction. Patients with BD have normal ffERGs, unlike in ARB, 247 
where progressive retinal dystrophy can develop from late childhood or early 248 
adolescence(Figure 2B). (Borman et al., 2011) However, rarely, the EOG may be borderline 249 
or within the reference range  in cases of bestrophinopathy, further highlighting phenotypic 250 
variability and the importance of genetic testing (Khan et al., 2018a). 251 
 252 
2.2.4. Imaging 253 
The subretinal vitelliform lesion presents with a high signal on FAF imaging and it is best 254 
visualised with OCT.(Ferrara et al., 2010) A common finding is SRF which waxes and wanes 255 
over time. Inherited causes of vitelliform lesion  can primarily be d istinguished from  non-256 
inherited acquired disorders due to the increased signal on FAF. The fibrosis that can occur 257 
in advanced BD has been described as resembling a “circus tent” due to its exaggerated 258 
height and unusual height -to-base ratio. (Kumar and Chatra, 2018)  OCT angiography 259 
(OCTA) has suggested this fibrosis to have a neovascular origin. (Shahzad and Siddiqui, 260 
2017) Fibrotic lesions appear hypoautofluorescent on FAF. OCTA is particularly useful in 261 
identifying CNV in vitelliform disorders including BD, where FFA can be very challenging to 262 
interpret.(Rahman et al., 2019) 263 
 264 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
11 
2.2.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 265 
Prognosis is often relatively good in BD. Acute visual loss and metamorphopsia, retinal 266 
haemorrhage, and intraretinal fluid should raise suspicion of CNV and investigation; SRF is 267 
unhelpful, given it is often observed in BD not complicated by CNV (thereby SRF is also not 268 
a useful indicator of CNV treatment response). Treatment with anti-vascular endothelial 269 
growth factor (VEGF) agents such as intravitreal bevacizumab has been found to be very 270 
effective, with improvement in structural and functional measurements, in contrast to 271 
observation alone.(Khan et al., 2017)  Unlike other causes of CNV, those associated with 272 
IRD often require limited numbers of injections, usually one or two are sufficient.  273 
Research avenues for BEST1-dominant disease are at present limited. Human iPSC 274 
(hiPSC)-derived RPE models from patients harbouring AD BEST1 variants, can potentially 275 
determine whether a gene augmentation approach would also be beneficial for dominant 276 
disease.(Guziewicz et al., 2018)  Canine models of ARB have been successfully rescued 277 
with AAV -mediated gene replacement, (Guziewicz et al., 2013)  (Wood et al., 2019)  and 278 
investigation of disease natural history in humans has identified a wide window for 279 
intervention (Casalino et al., 2020).  280 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
12 
2.3. X-linked retinoschisis (XLRS) 281 
XLRS (RS1, OMIM 312700)  is the most common form of juvenile -onset macular  282 
degeneration in males. (Rahman et al., 2019) , and is usually associated with genera lised 283 
inner retinal dysfunction on ERG. 284 
 285 
2.3.1. Molecular Genetics 286 
Disease-causing variants in RS1 (OMIM #312700) underlie XLRS, with the encoded cell-287 
surface protein, Retinoschisin-1, expressed in photoreceptors and bipolar cells, having a 288 
role in retinal cell adhesion. RS1 disease-associated variants disrupt the subunit assembly 289 
of the protein and lead to alteration of normal retinal cell adhesion, thus resulting in splitting 290 
of the neural layers of the retina. Carrier females lack a clinical retinal phenotype, in contrast 291 
to many other XL-retinal disorders (De Silva et al., 2020).   292 
 293 
2.3.2. Clinical Assessment 294 
XLRS typically presents in the first to second decade in a variety of ways, including with 295 
poor visual acuity, strabismus, a nisometropia, and 'unexplained visual loss'. The hallmark 296 
feature of XLRS is the “spoke -wheel” folds of the macula (macular schisis) , although not 297 
always present, and evolving into non-specific macular atrophy in some older men (Vincent 298 
et al., 2013) . Peripheral retinal changes are observed in approxima tely 50% of males, 299 
including schisis (with inner or outer retinal holes), pigmentary disturbance, white 300 
spiculations, metallic sheen, neovascularisation  and vitreous veils  in some cases . An 301 
increased risk of rhegmatogenous retinal detachment (RD), or vitre ous haemorrhage (VH) 302 
has been reported for patients with peripheral retinoschisis. (Fahim et al., 2017)  Bullous 303 
XLRS can be congenital or develop soon after birth, and it can be complicated later in life 304 
by RD, which may be rhegmatogenous, tractional or a Coats -like exudative 305 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
13 
detachment.(Hinds et al., 2018) The development of macular atrophy may lead to slow VA 306 
loss in older adults.(Georgiou et al., 2022b, Hahn et al., 2021)   307 
 308 
2.3.3. Electrophysiology 309 
The ffERG in XLRS typically shows a preserved a-wave and a reduced b:a-wave ratio in 310 
dark-adapted bright flash recordings, also known as an “electronegative ERG” (typically in 311 
cases where the b:a ratio is less than 1) ,(Jiang and Mahroo, 2021)  with light adapted 312 
recordings indicating generalised cone system dysfunction in almost all cases. The degree 313 
of inner retinal dysfunction varies from mild to severe, with widest variability including mildest 314 
retinal dysfunction associated with missense rather than nonsense and other severe 315 
variants (Vincent et al., 2013) . Pattern ERG P50 is typically abnormal, in keeping with 316 
macular dysfunction, irrespective of the variant. ffERG evidence of inner retinal dysfunction 317 
can suggest the diagnosis in the absence of typical clinical signs (e.g., (Robson et al., 2009) 318 
(Tsang et al., 2007). 319 
 320 
2.3.4. Imaging 321 
The identification of m acular schisis can be challenging on clinical examination, making 322 
multimodal imaging invaluable. Splitting of the inner and outer retinal layers can be readily 323 
identified with OCT , and a spoke -wheel appearance of areas of high - and low - signal 324 
intensity is observed with FAF imaging (Figure 3).(Georgiou et al., 2022b) Rarely, macular 325 
OCT can be normal/near normal, and peripheral changes and/or ERG are important for 326 
clinical diagnosis. Less common vascular abnormalities such as vascular sheathing and 327 
neovascularisation can be imaged with u ltra-wide field imaging with FFA and 328 
OCTA.(Berenberg et al., 2016)  329 
 330 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
14 
2.3.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 331 
Carbonic anhydrase inhibitors (CAIs) have been shown to be sometimes useful in managing 332 
schisis in XLRS (Apushkin and Fishman, 2006, Andreuzzi et al., 2017). There has also been 333 
a disconnect reported between VA improvement and lack of structural change. (Pennesi et 334 
al., 2018)  335 
 Intravitreal RS1 gene replacement in knockout mice has resulted in func tional ERG 336 
improvement.(Ou et al., 2015, Byrne et al., 2014) This has led to two phase I/II XLRS gene 337 
therapy trials (NCT02416622 and NCT02317887) delivering gene replacement intravitreally. 338 
The former trial has ceased due to ocular inflammation associated with intravitreal delivery 339 
and lack of robust efficacy signals, whilst the latter has added additional agents  to the 340 
standard oral steroids used in subretinal gene supplementation trials to address the uveitis-341 
related adverse events. A subretinal gene supplementation approach has been developed 342 
and is in clinical trial (NCT05878860). However, the difficulty is that XLRS is predominantly 343 
relatively stationary, with patients often also not severely visually impaired, both making the 344 
demonstration of clinically meaningful benefit very challenging (Georgiou et al., 2022b).  345 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
15 
2.4. PRPH2-associated retinal dystrophy 346 
2.4.1. Molecular Genetics 347 
PRPH2 (OMIM #169150) variants most often present as p attern dystrophy ( PD), an 348 
autosomal dominant condition, with variable phenotype including most commonly butterfly-349 
shaped pigment dystrophy and reticular dystrophy. Other genes such as BEST1, CTNNA1 350 
and PYGM have also been associated with PD phenotypes.(Seddon et al., 2001, Mahroo et 351 
al., 2019) PRPH2 encodes peripherin-2, a multimeric structural protein that establishes and 352 
maintains the morphology of photoreceptor outer segment (OS) discs. (Farjo and Naash, 353 
2006) Abnormal peripherin-2 found in patients with PD results in an ultra-structural alteration 354 
of OS discs with an abnormal whorl -like arrangement histopathologically. (Wickham et al., 355 
2009) The p.(Arg172Trp) PRPH2 genotype can be associated with incomplete penetrance 356 
and generalised retinal involvement (Michaelides et al., 2005a). 357 
 358 
2.4.2. Clinical Assessment 359 
Patients present in the 4th to 5th decade, either following routine optometry review or having 360 
noticed mild limitation in central vision. PD is usually associated with a good prognosis, 361 
unless rarely complicated by CNV (highly responsive to anti -VEGF agent s). PD is 362 
characterised by variable distributions of pigment deposition at the level of the RPE. Several 363 
different fundus appearances have been described based on the variable patterns of 364 
deposition, including butterfly-shaped pigment dystrophy and reticular dystrophy.  365 
 366 
2.4.3. Electrophysiology 367 
Full-field ERGs in PD are generally normal or only mildly subnormal, with PERG or multifocal 368 
ERG ( mfERG) evidence of macular dysfunction  and EOG evidence of generalised RPE 369 
dysfunction in some cases  (Tanner et al., 2021) . PRPH2-associated retinal disorders can 370 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
16 
be associated with more severe disease and different forms of retinal dystrophy, including 371 
rod-cone, cone, cone-rod and inner retinal patterns of generalised dysfunction, evident on 372 
ffERG testing.  373 
 374 
2.4.4. Imaging 375 
The deposits in PD are typically hyperautofluorescent on FAF , and may result in a 376 
characteristic speckled pattern .(Renner et al., 2009)  As these changes are at the level of 377 
RPE, subretinal hyper-reflective material is seen on OCT (Figure 4A) . 378 
 379 
2.4.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 380 
Successful integration and material transfer of donor - or stem cell -derived cone 381 
photoreceptors in Prph2rd2/rd2 murine models of the disease is promising. (Waldron et al., 382 
2018)  383 
 384 
 385 
  386 
Journal Pre-proof

2.5. Sorsby fundus dystrophy 387 
Sorsby fundus dystrophy (SFD) is a rare autosomal dominant drusen-associated MD.  388 
 389 
2.5.1. Molecular Genetics 390 
SFD is associated with missense variants in the tissue inhibitor of metalloproteinase -3 391 
(TIMP3) gene (OMIM #188826). These substitutions usually create a new cysteine residue, 392 
with p.(Ser204Cys) being the commonest. TIMP3 is an inhibitor of matrix 393 
metalloproteinases, which play an important role in the regulation of extracellular matrix 394 
(ECM) turnover. Mutant TIMP3 accumulates within Bruch 's membrane, disrupting 395 
homeostasis of ECM remodelling and interfering with the normal critical functions of Bruch's 396 
membrane, choroid and RPE. (Stohr and Anand -Apte, 2012) Recently, variants within the 397 
signal peptide region of TIMP3 have been shown to give rise to an autosomal dominant 398 
macular dystrophy with some differences from typical SFD.(Guan et al., 2022) 399 
 400 
2.5.2. Clinical Assessment 401 
Early signs of SFD are the macular yellowish-grey drusen-like deposits at the level of Bruch's 402 
membrane which preferentially accumulate along the temporal arcades , likely related to 403 
impaired transport of retinoid pre-cursors from the RPE. Patients may be asymptomatic at 404 
this stage, however difficulty with dark adaptation can be an early symptom.(Rahman et al., 405 
2020) The deposits progress over time to include the central macula. Visual loss can be 406 
secondary to slow atrophic degeneration at the macula, which can also extend peripherally, 407 
usually in the fifth dec ade of life. CNV is a common complication, often resulting in severe 408 
VA loss.(Mohla et al., 2016) 409 
 410 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
18 
2.5.3. Electrophysiology 411 
Full-field ERGs are usually normal. Macular dysfunction may be evident on PERG or 412 
mfERG.  413 
 414 
2.5.4. Imaging 415 
FAF imaging may identify a broad ill -defined increase in signal in the peripheral macula in 416 
early disease, with subretinal drusenoid deposits - reticular pseudodrusen, that spare the 417 
central fovea, clearly depicted on infra-red imaging (Figure 4B). OCT can identify drusen-418 
like deposits and delineate Bruch ’s membrane (BM) thickening; and is valuable in the 419 
diagnosis of CNV. Both in SFD and EFEMP1-associated autosomal dominant drusen, OCT 420 
assessment of macular structure identifies a separation of the usually single, hyperreflective 421 
line thought to represent BM and the overlying RPE (Khan et al., 2020). Early CNV changes 422 
can be captured with OCTA, without the need for FFA (Mohla et al., 2016). 423 
 424 
2.5.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 425 
Currently, no animal or cell culture model capable of recapitulating human SFD is available. 426 
Prompt use of anti -VEGF injections may improve outcome for SFD complicated by CNV. 427 
Early attempts at treating SFD involved oral vitamin A at 50,000  IU/day, with a short‐term 428 
reversal of night blindness in patients at early stages of disease.(Jacobson et al., 1995) Due 429 
to the potential toxicity of long term high dose vitamin A and reports of lack of efficacy at 430 
lower doses (15,000  IU/day) in advanced disease, vitamin A is not a widely used 431 
treatment.(Gliem et al., 2015)  Patient-derived iPSC ‐RPE models may provide a suitable 432 
platform for investigating SFD.(Galloway et al., 2017)   433 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
19 
2.6. EFEMP1-associated Autosomal Dominant Drusen 434 
Similar to SFD, EFEMP1-associated autosomal dominant drusen (ADD) is a rare MD, with 435 
drusenoid deposits in the macula.(Khan et al., 2016, Khan et al., 2020) 436 
 437 
2.6.1. Molecular Genetics 438 
ADD is caused by a single missense variant, p.(Arg345Trp), in EFEMP1 (OMIM #601548). 439 
EGF-containing fibulin -like extracellular matrix protein -1 (EFEMP1) is a member of the 440 
fibulin family that encodes for fibulin-3 (F3). The p.(Arg345Trp) substitution in F3, results in 441 
a sub -RPE membranous accumulation of debris associated with signs of complement 442 
activation and RPE atrophy in a mouse model of ADD. (Marmorstein et al., 2007) Efemp1 443 
knockout mice do not develop sub -RPE deposits following exposure to environmental 444 
stressors (high fat diet diet/laser or high fat diet/cigarette smoke), which may suggest that 445 
deletion of Efemp1 may have a protective role in ADD.(Stanton et al., 2017) 446 
 447 
2.6.2. Clinical Assessment 448 
Drusen-like deposits in ADD at the macula may be in a radiating  pattern ('Malattia 449 
leventinese') or honeycomb -like appearance  (Doyne honeycomb retinal dystrophy 450 
(MIM 126600)). The drusen in ADD typically abut the optic nerve head. (Michaelides et al., 451 
2006b) Visual loss may occur in ADD due to the development of a variable degree of central 452 
atrophy, or can be complicated by CNV. (Michaelides et al., 2006b)  There is marked inter - 453 
and intra -familial variability observed in terms of retinal appearance, severity and 454 
progression.(Michaelides et al., 2006b) . The deposits are sub -RPE (as with conventional 455 
drusen) rather than subretinal. 456 
 457 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
20 
2.6.3. Electrophysiology 458 
Full-field ERGs are typically normal, although there are reports of abnormally increased 459 
flicker ERG peak times in some older individuals with genetically un confirmed disease 460 
(Gerber et al., 2003). There may be PERG or mfERG evidence of macular dysfunction. 461 
 462 
2.6.4. Imaging 463 
The drusen -like deposit s in ADD are often hyperautofluorescent on FAF , in contrast to 464 
drusen in AMD  which are variable on FAF .(Evans et al., 1997, Michaelides et al., 2006b)  465 
Drusen associated with increased signal on blue-FAF are generally more likely to be  466 
associated with monogenic inherited disease and so FAF imaging is thereby a useful 467 
screening tool. Drusen-like deposits are seen as hyper -reflective thickening of the RPE -468 
Bruch's membrane complex,(Souied et al., 2006) with disrupted photoreceptor integrity on 469 
OCT, (Figure 4C).(Gerth et al., 2009) CNV in ADD can be diagnosed with OCTA .(Serra et 470 
al., 2017)  471 
 472 
2.6.5. Avenues of Intervention 473 
Similar to SFD, no animal or cell culture model capable of recapitulating human ADD is 474 
available, with patient -derived iPSC ‐RPE models also being explored for investigating 475 
ADD.(Galloway et al., 2017)  476 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
21 
3. CONE AND CONE-ROD DYSTROPHIES 477 
Progressive COD/CORD typically present with progressive loss of central vision, colour 478 
vision disturbance and photophobia.(Gill et al., 2019, Guimaraes et al., 2020). COD/CORD 479 
are characterised by cone photoreceptor degeneration, which may be followed by 480 
subsequent rod photoreceptor loss. (Michaelides et al., 2006a)  We discuss  the most 481 
common genotypes and genotypes with distinct features.  482 
 483 
Electrophysiology 484 
A delayed 30 -Hz flicker ERG implicit time, which selectively assesses cone -driven 485 
responses, is usually the earliest ERG finding in COD/CORD. (Hamel, 2007)  Scotopic 486 
function is preserved in early disease, but is usually affected in late disease; indicating rod 487 
system involvement with disease progression. (Langwinska-Wosko et al., 2015)  In one 488 
genotype of CORD, KCNV2-associated retinopathy, the ERG findings are characteristic and 489 
diagnostic and are presented individually.(Guimaraes et al., 2020, Georgiou et al., 2020d) 490 
 491 
3.1. Autosomal Recessive ABCA4-associated COD/CORD  492 
3.1.1. Molecular Genetics 493 
Variants in the ABCA4 gene account for approximately 60 to 65% of autosomal recessive 494 
COD/CORD cases. (Gill et al., 2019)  The function of ABCA4 has been described in the 495 
STGD1 section under the MD section. 496 
 497 
3.1.2. Electrophysiology 498 
The electrophysiology of ABCA4-associated dystrophies is described in the STGD1 section.  499 
 500 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
22 
3.1.3. Clinical Assessment 501 
Symptomatic onset usually occurs in childhood or early adulthood with a central scotoma 502 
and progressive macular atrophy.(Fujinami et al., 2015b, Tanna et al., 2017b, Tanna et al., 503 
2018) In ABCA4 COD/CORD (OMIM 601691), fundoscopy may initially reveal a normal 504 
fundus or mild retinal abnormalities (such as loss of foveal reflex), whereas peripheral 505 
degenerative changes occur in later dise ase.(Westeneng-van Haaften et al., 2012)  506 
Diagnosis can therefore be delayed unless FAF or OCT imaging is undertaken.(Fujinami et 507 
al., 2015b) Patients with rod involvement at presentation have a worse prognosis.(Fujinami 508 
et al., 2013b) Macular atrophy and yellow–white flecks at the level of the RPE at the posterior 509 
pole are found in the majority of patients. (Tanna et al., 2017b) Progression can be rapid in 510 
early childhood; however, cases where progression is very rapid  may raise the alternative 511 
possibility of CLN3-associated syndromic disease (Batten disease).(Wright et al., 2020) 512 
 513 
3.1.4. Imaging 514 
FAF findings include a bull’s -eye maculopathy -like appearance with yellow -white retinal 515 
flecks and increasing macular atrophy over time;(Fujinami et al., 2013b, Michaelides et al., 516 
2007) whilst OCT reveals loss of outer retinal architecture at the central macula.(Strauss et 517 
al., 2016b) Longitudinal increase in abnormal AF regions correlates with both visual function 518 
decline and abnormal cone spacing on AOSLO. (Chen et al., 2011)  OCT can be used for 519 
quantifying the rate of ellipsoid zone (EZ) loss and investigating disease natural 520 
history.(Tanna et al., 2019, Cai et al., 2017) However, cone mosaic abnormalities are known 521 
to precede abnormal psychophysical testing and FAF.(Song et al., 2015) 522 
 523 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
23 
3.1.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 524 
For detailed presentation on therapeutic approaches for ABCA4 disease, see the STGD1 525 
section under MD.  526 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
24 
3.2. Autosomal Dominant GUCA1A-associated COD/CORD 527 
3.2.1. Molecular Genetics 528 
GUCA1A (OMIM 600364) variants account for approximately 15% of AD COD/CORD. (Gill 529 
et al., 2019)  GUCA1A is a four -exon gene encoding GCAP1  (guanylyl cyclase activating 530 
protein), which is required for RetGC activation and cGMP regeneration. (Subbaraya et al., 531 
1994) GCAP1 is regulated in a Ca 2+-dependent manner and contains three Ca 2+-binding 532 
EF-hand motifs. Gain-in-function variants in GUCA1A; p.(Tyr99Cys),(Downes et al., 2001) 533 
p.(Glu155Gly)(Wilkie et al., 2001) and p.(Asp100Gly),(Nong et al., 2014, Palczewski et al., 534 
2004) result in structural alteration of those motifs. These result in persistent stimulation of 535 
RetGC, excess cGMP levels in the dark, and photoreceptor apoptosis secondary to Ca 2+ 536 
dysregulation.(Kitiratschky et al., 2009, Stockman et al., 2014b)  537 
 538 
3.2.2. Genotype-Phenotype Correlations 539 
The phenotypic variability in patients harbouring identical GUCA1A mutations is noteworthy. 540 
Michaelides et al.  demonstrated the p.(Tyr99Cys) missense variant resulting in three 541 
different dominantly-inherited phenotypes in a single n on-consanguineous pedigree: COD, 542 
CORD, and isolated MD. (Michaelides et al., 2005e)  Similarly, another study identified a 543 
p.(Arg120Leu) substitution as the cause of clinically heterogeneous MD.(Chen et al., 2017) 544 
The p.(Pro50Leu) variant in GUCA1A, previously thought to be pathogenic,(Downes et al., 545 
2001) was shown late r to be a likely benign variant , as the originally reported family was 546 
found to have a different cause for their disease , namely a pathogenic variant in RPGR. 547 
(Mahroo et al., 2020) 548 
 549 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
25 
3.2.3. Electrophysiology 550 
Full-field ERGs show reduced light-adapted (LA) cone-mediated responses, with some but 551 
not all, showing preserved peak times, consistent with a loss of cone function rather than 552 
generalised cone dysfunction (Downes et al., 2001). It is highlighted that most other CODs 553 
are associated with LA ERG reduction and delay and that subnormal responses of normal 554 
timing are an unusual feature that may inform diagnosis. Rod function may remain normal, 555 
although dysfunction may occur later in disease natural history in COD, or be reduced at 556 
presentation in CORD.  557 
 Cones are the primary photoreceptor class affected, possibly due to greater GCAP1 558 
expression in cones compared with rods. (Kachi et al., 1999)  The combination of a flicker 559 
ERG delay, an electronegative On - response and the unusual shape of the Off - ERG 560 
waveform distinguishes these cases from those with autosomal dominant cone dystrophy 561 
due to GUCA1A mutation, often associated with a reduced cone ERG of normal timing with 562 
subnormal On- and Off- ERG components (Downes et al., 2001). The latter study speculated 563 
that these abnormalities could result from impair ed transduction kinetics, but there is also 564 
evidence of weak RetGC expression in the outer plexiform layer (Kachi et al., 1999) , and 565 
histological evidence of synaptic disruption in cases of CORD (Gregory-Evans et al., 1998). 566 
 567 
3.2.4. Clinical Assessment 568 
Patients usually present in the second and third decade of life with reduced central vision, 569 
photophobia, and generalised dyschromatopsia. GUCA1A-associated COD/CORD has 570 
variable fundoscopy findings, ranging from mild RPE disturbance to extensive macular 571 
atrophy.  572 
 573 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
26 
3.2.5. Imaging 574 
FAF is useful in investigating macular abnormalities, although both areas of 575 
hypoautofluorescence and hyperautofluorescence have correlated with retinal atrophy; an 576 
increased signal at the fovea may be seen in early disease. (Michaelides et al., 2006a, 577 
Michaelides et al., 2005e, Chen et al., 2017)  A variable degree of EZ disruption over the 578 
fovea can be observed, as well as macular thinning  (Figure 5A).(Tsokolas et al., 2018)  579 
Cellular variability between patients harbouring the same variant  is identified with 580 
AOSLO.(Song et al., 2018)  581 
 582 
3.2.6. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 583 
A murine model of disease with the gain of function p.(Tyr99Cys) variant in GUCA1A, has 584 
shown significantly increased photoreceptor survival following RNA interference -based 585 
gene silencing. (Jiang et al., 2011)  Sleeping with the lights on is advocated by some 586 
clinicians for preventing accumulation of cGMP, which otherwise occurs at night and is 587 
believed to cause photoreceptor damage.(Stockman et al., 2014b)   588 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
27 
3.3. Autosomal Dominant GUCY2D-associated COD/CORD 589 
3.3.1. Molecular Genetics 590 
GUCY2D (OMIM 600179) variants can cause both COD, CORD, and LCA.(Xiao et al., 2011, 591 
Zhao et al., 2013, Hahn et al., 20 22). GUCY2D-associated COD/CORD is an AD disease, 592 
in contrast to LCA which is recessive. (Bouzia et al., 2020)  GUCY2D encodes the 593 
photoreceptor enzyme guanylate cyclase 2D (GC -E; RetGC), a component of the 594 
phototransduction cascade, that is regulated by intracellular Ca 2+-sensor proteins such as 595 
GCAP1. Variants in GUCY2D account for approximately 30% of all cases.(Gill et al., 2019) 596 
Together, GUCA1A, PRPH2  and GUCY2D, account for approximately 65 to 70% of 597 
autosomal dominant COD/CORD cases.(Gill et al., 2019)  598 
 599 
3.3.2. Clinical Assessment 600 
Age of disease onset varies, presenting with reduced visual acuity, nystagmus, photophobia, 601 
and decreased colour vision. (Xiao et al., 2011, Small et al., 2008, Gregory -Evans et al., 602 
2000) Fundoscopy findings are varied, ranging from mild RPE changes to extensive macular 603 
atrophy, observed in older individuals.(Small et al., 2008, Gregory-Evans et al., 2000)  604 
 605 
3.3.3. Electrophysiology 606 
ERG studies characteristically show reduced cone single -flash and flicker amplitudes. 607 
Negative scotopic electroretinogram can be observed early in the disease and greater rod 608 
dysfunction may occur over time.(Gregory-Evans et al., 2000, Zobor et al., 2014, Nasser et 609 
al., 2019) Most typically show LA30Hz ERG delay (unlike some cases of GUCA1A) and 610 
reduction. 611 
 612 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
28 
3.3.4. Imaging  613 
FAF imaging usually shows central hypoautofluorescence and a surrounding ring of 614 
hyperautofluorescence (Figure 5B).(Gregory-Evans et al., 2000)  A variable degree of EZ 615 
disruption over the fovea can be observed, as well as macular thinning. (Tsokolas et al., 616 
2018) 617 
 618 
3.3.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 619 
Somatic gene editing using AAV -delivered CRISPR/Cas9 has been used to edit the 620 
GUCY2D early coding sequence in mouse and macaque photoreceptors in vivo, thereby 621 
knocking out retGC1 expression, and demonstrating promising results, altering both retinal 622 
function and structure. (McCullough et al., 2018)  Advancement in GUCY2D-LCA clinical 623 
therapeutics investigations are discussed under the LCA section.   624 
Journal Pre-proof

3.4. Autosomal Dominant PRPH2-associated CORD  625 
3.4.1. Molecular Genetics 626 
PRPH2 COD/CORD (OMIM 179605) accounts for approximately 20% of autosomal 627 
dominant COD/CORD.(Gill et al., 2019) PRPH2 is a five-exon gene encoding peripherin-2, 628 
a cell surface glycoprotein in the outer segment with an essential role in disc 629 
morphogenesis.(Ma et al., 1995) Interactions of peripherin-2 with ROM1 and glutamic acid-630 
rich domains of CNG channels support its function in disc stabilisation and maintenance of 631 
rim curvature.(Travis et al., 1991) CORD-associated variants in PRPH2 can be attributed to 632 
the region encoding the second intradiscal loop between its four transmembrane 633 
components. This contains cysteine residues essen tial for intra -protein folding and inter -634 
protein interactions.(Loewen and Molday, 2000)  Identified missense variants in this region 635 
include p.(Asn244His), (Nakazawa et al., 1996a)  p.(Val200Glu)(Nakazawa et al., 1996b) , 636 
and p.(Arg172Trp).(Michaelides et al., 2005a) 637 
 638 
3.4.2. Genotype-Phenotype Correlations 639 
Certain genotype -phenotype correlations have been observed . Families harbouring the 640 
p.(Asn244His) or p.(Val200Glu) variant present with early central RPE atrophy that 641 
advances peripherally with disease progression, with little intrafamilial variability.(Nakazawa 642 
et al., 1996a, Nakazawa et al., 1996b)  In contrast, clinical phenotypes associated with 643 
p.(Arg172Trp) can vary substantially, ranging from non -penetrance to severe CORD, RP,  644 
LCA/EOSRD, macular dystrophy, and central areolar choroidal dystrophy (CACD).(Downes 645 
et al., 1999, Michaelides et al., 2005a, Gamundi et al., 2007, Anand et al., 2009) . The 646 
missense variant p.(Gly167Asp) is also associated with phenotypic variability; causing both 647 
autosomal dominant rod -cone dystrophy (Ba-Abbad et al., 2019) , and butterfly -shaped 648 
pigment dystrophy.(Nichols et al., 1993)  649 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
30 
 650 
3.4.3. Clinical Assessment 651 
Patients usually present in the second to third decade with reduced central vision, 652 
photophobia, and nyctalopia. Fundoscopic appearance ranges from a bull’s-eye 653 
maculopathy to macular atrophy , with rare instances of EOSRD .(Georgiou et al., 2021a)  654 
CACD is characterised by a late onset central cone dystrophy.(Boon et al., 2009) 655 
 656 
3.4.4. Imaging 657 
A characteristic speckled macular appearance on FAF is demonstrated in most patients  658 
(Figure 5C).(Michaelides et al., 2005a) AOSLO imaging in p.(Arg172Trp)-associated CORD 659 
revealed increased cone spacing throughout the macula with corresponding loss of outer 660 
retinal structures on OCT. (Duncan et al., 2011)  However, intrafamilial analysis of 661 
p.(Arg172Gln)-associated disease has shown marked variability.(Gocho et al., 2016) 662 
 663 
3.4.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 664 
At present, whilst there are no specific proven treatments, several avenues of intervention 665 
show promise. The Prph2rd2/rd2 murine model has shown significantly increased 666 
photoreceptor survival following gene replacement therapy. (Sarra et al., 2001)  Promising 667 
transplantation success of stem cell - and donor-derived cones, with both integration and 668 
material transfer, has also been reported in the aforementioned murine degenerative 669 
model.(Waldron et al., 2018) A pharmacological approach, with inhibition of the overactive 670 
poly-ADP-ribose polymerase (PARP) with the PARP inhibitor PJ34, has demonstrated a 671 
decrease in the levels of poly -ADP-ribosylation and photoreceptor cell death, in this same 672 
model.(Sahaboglu et al., 2017)  673 
Journal Pre-proof

3.5. Autosomal Dominant PROM1-associated CORD  674 
3.5.1. Molecular Genetics 675 
Variants in prominin 1 ( PROM1) can cause both AR and AD retina l degeneration, with a 676 
wide variety of phenotypes. Mutant prominin 1 found in patients with macular degeneration 677 
is proven to disrupt photoreceptor disk morphogenesis in mice.(Yang et al., 2008) PROM1 678 
is expressed in a wide variety of tissues (Arrigoni et al., 2011). 679 
 680 
3.5.2. Genotype-Phenotype Correlations 681 
The PROM1 variant p.(Arg373Cys) causes an AD bull's-eye maculopathy associated with 682 
rod, rod -cone, and macular dystrophy  (Michaelides et al., 2010a) . Dominant CORD, 683 
previously assigned to a missense variant in RIMS1, was fully explained by co-inheritance 684 
of the aforementioned  well-characterized PROM1 dominant allele : c.1118 C>T, 685 
p.(Arg373Cys) (Martin-Gutierrez et al., 2022) . Recessive variants cause a severe early -686 
onset panretinal degeneration. 687 
 688 
3.5.3. Clinical Assessment 689 
AD PROM1-retinopathy can present with MD, with RPE mottling in younger subjects, 690 
progressing to typical bull’s eye maculopathy  over time, with the development of macular 691 
atrophy in older patients. RP  features are also common . The electrophysiological findings 692 
can be variable both within and between families (Michaelides et al., 2010a, Schließleder et 693 
al., 2023) 694 
 695 
3.5.4. Imaging 696 
Initial pathological changes include the presence of hyperautofluorescent spots on FAF 697 
imaging, while later stages demonstrate discrete areas of atrophy. In all patients, thinning of 698 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
32 
the outer retinal layers on OCT with varying degrees of atrophy can be detected depending 699 
on disease-causing variants and age. Cone density has been quantified with AOSLO both 700 
in central and/or at different eccentricities from the fovea (Schließleder et al., 2023). 701 
 702 
3.5.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 703 
A prospective natural h istory study (NCT02410122, ProgStar-4) for patients with PROM1-704 
disease investigates the natural history, in order to facilitate future clinical trials  (Strauss et 705 
al., 2018). No established treatment or trial exist s currently, but gene augmentation  alone 706 
for AR disease, or  combined with silencing of the dominant variant effect may be an 707 
approach. 708 
 709 
3.5.6. Electrophysiology 710 
Electrophysiological findings are variable between and within families, ranging from isolated 711 
macular dysfunction (normal ffERGs with PERG P50 reduction) to generalised rod, rod-cone 712 
or severe rod and cone photoreceptor dysfunction (Michaelides et al., 2010a).  713

3.6. X-Linked RPGR-associated COD/CORD 714 
3.6.1. Molecular Genetics 715 
Variants in RPGR (MIM *312610) constitute the most prevalent monogenic cause of X-linked 716 
COD/CORD (approximately 70%). (Gill et al., 2019)   RPGR is a nineteen -exon gene that 717 
gives rise to two al ternatively spliced retinal isoforms, encoded by exons 1 -19 and 1-15 (+ 718 
part of intron 15) respectively. (Ghosh et al., 2010)  The latter isoform, also known as exon 719 
open reading frame 15 (ORF15), is the most highly expressed retinal RNA isoform and a 720 
mutational hotspot, accounting for most X -linked COD and CORD cases. (Vervoort et al., 721 
2000, Michaelides et al., 2006a)  The function of the C -terminal ORF15 sequence requires 722 
further elucidation, but is implicated in intraflagellar protein transport in view of its localisation 723 
at the photoreceptor connecting cilium.(Khanna et al., 2005)  724 
 725 
3.6.2. Genotype-Phenotype Correlations 726 
Most disease-causing variants in RPGR result in RP,(Shu et al., 2007) but those leading to 727 
COD/CORD are preferentially located at the 3’ end of the ORF15 region. (Ebenezer et al., 728 
2005) However, identical intrafamilial sequence variants in RPGR may lead to d istinctly 729 
different phenotypes.(Tee et al., 2016a)  A longitudinal study reported significantly higher 730 
levels of legal blindness among RPGR-associated COD/CORD compared to RPGR-731 
associated RP by the age of 40 years, in which high myopia was predictive of faster visual 732 
decline.(Talib et al., 2018) 733 
 734 
3.6.3. Clinical Assessment 735 
RPGR-associated CORD is characterised by central visual loss, mild photophobia, and 736 
myopia, and pres ents in the second to fourth decade in affected males. (Thiadens et al., 737 
2011)  738 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
34 
 739 
3.6.4. Imaging 740 
FAF imaging often reveals parafoveal rings of increased  signal in RPGR-associated 741 
COD/CORD. Unlike RP, these increase in size with disease progression and are inversely 742 
related to ERG amplitude. (Robson et al., 2008a, Tee et al., 2017b)  OCT shows marked 743 
disruption of the EZ, progressing over time (Figure 5D). 744 
 745 
3.6.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 746 
The advancement in clinical trials for  RPGR-RP are discussed in the rod -cone dystrophy 747 
section in detail.   748

 749 
3.7. KCNV2-associated COD/CORD 750 
3.7.1. Molecular Genetics 751 
The KCNV2 gene (MIM *607604) encodes Kv8.2, a voltage -gated potassium channel 752 
subunit that binds in heterotetramer complexes with Kv2.1, and  acts as  a modulator by 753 
shifting the activation range of the K+ channels in photoreceptor inner segments (Wu et al., 754 
2006).   755 
 756 
3.7.2. Clinical Assessment 757 
KCNV2-retinopathy is a childhood-onset disease, with >50% of patients symptomatic before 758 
3 years of age and 1/10 symptomatic at birth. Best-corrected visual acuity (BCVA) is reduced 759 
universally from early age and slowly worsens with age. Photophobia and night vision 760 
difficulties are present in > 1/3 patients at baseline, and high myopia (>6.0 D) is common 761 
(>30%); with this combination of symptoms and signs raising suspicion of this disorder  762 
(Georgiou et al., 2020d, Michaelides et al., 2005b) . Patients with missense alterations had 763 
better BCVA and greater structural integrity (de Guimaraes et al., 2023).  764 
 765 
3.7.3. Electrophysiology 766 
In KCNV2-associated retinopathy, ffERGs are diagnostic and show pathognomonic ERG 767 
abnormalities. There is evidence of generalised cone system dysfunction with unusual rod 768 
system involvement, whereby responses to the dimmest flashes are abnormally small and 769 
delayed, and responses to strong flashes relatively large, with distinctive waveform distortion 770 
and broadening of the a -wave trough (e.g., Robson et al., 2010; Georgiou et al., 2020d). 771 
Pattern ERG P50 is invariably undetectable, irrespective of the macular appearance and in 772 
keeping with se vere macular dysfunction. The rate of age -associated ERG reduction 773 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
36 
resembles that seen in healthy subjects. The disorder was historically termed “cone 774 
dystrophy with supernormal rod ERG” although not associated with greater than normal rod 775 
function (Georgiou et al., 2020d, Stockman et al., 2014a, Robson et al., 2010). 776 
 777 
3.7.4. Imaging 778 
KCNV2-associated retinopathy is a slowly progressive disease with early retinal changes, 779 
which are predominantly symmetric al between eyes, both in terms of OCT and FAF 780 
imaging.(Georgiou et al., 2021d)  OCT can show a variable degree of EZ disruption and 781 
atrophic changes in a subset of older individuals with the disease (Figure 6)(Sergouniotis 782 
et al., 2012) . FAF can have an equally divers e presentation ranging from a normal 783 
autofluorescence pattern, increased foveal signal, decreased foveal signal, perifoveal ring 784 
of increased signal or areas of no signal secondary to atrophy. The identification of a single 785 
OCT or FAF measurement as an endpoint to determine progression that applies to all 786 
patients may be challenging, although outer nuclear layer  (ONL) thickness has been 787 
identified as a potential biomarker for future interventions. (Georgiou et al., 2021d) 788 
 789 
3.7.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 790 
Imaging findings suggest a potential window for intervention until 40 years of age.(Georgiou 791 
et al., 2021d)  The wide window and the small size of the gene that can be readily 792 
accommodated by AAV, make the disease a potential target for gene therapy. (Guimaraes 793 
et al., 2020)  794

4. ROD-CONE DYSTROPHIES 795 
Rod-cone dystrophies (RCD) are a variable group of inherited retinal conditions, both in 796 
terms of phenotype and genotype,(Tee et al., 2016b) with a prevalence of 1/3000 - 1/4000 797 
in the general population.(Hartong et al., 2006, Verbakel et al., 2018) Herein we present the 798 
imaging findings of retinitis pigmentosa  (RP), and two other conditions that affect the rods  799 
more than (or before) the cones, namely Enhanced S-Cone Syndrome (ESCS) and Bietti 800 
crystalline corneoretinal dystrophy (the latter may affect the RPE before the photoreceptors). 801 
We have prioritised RP /RCD genotypes with on -going or up -coming therapeutic 802 
interventions for specific discussion. 803 
 804 
4.1. Retinitis Pigmentosa (RP) 805 
 806 
4.1.1. Clinical Assessment 807 
RP is the most common IRD phenotype. RP is characteri sed by nyctalopia and gradual 808 
constriction of the visual field, with eventual loss of central vision, progressing to legal 809 
blindness.(Tee et al., 2016a, Fahim, 2018) . RP can be inherited as an AD, autosomal 810 
recessive (AR) or X-linked (XL) trait.  811 
 812 
4.1.2. Imaging  813 
Due to t he large number of genes involved (>100 genes) and the lack of distinct/specific 814 
genotype-phenotype correlations for the vast majority, an overview of retinal imaging will be 815 
presented for RP and not for each specific genotype. Areas of atrophied RPE/photoreceptor 816 
cell loss have decreased AF because of the lack of lipofuscin, and are observed to a greater 817 
extent in the mid-periphery (Figure 7A). Wide-field FAF can better evaluate the full extent 818 
of peripheral atrophy in RP. Increased AF is often observed in the form of a concentric ring 819 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
38 
around the macula  (Robson et al., 2003, Robson et al., 2008a) , and represents areas of 820 
dysfunctional/degenerating photoreceptors.(Robson et al., 2003) The hyperautofluorescent 821 
ring demarcates the area of central viable retina from the surrounding atrophic retina, and 822 
is observed to constrict over time, in contrast to CORD, where it is seen to expand (Robson 823 
et al., 2008b, Robson et al., 2011)). The ring size and width has been reported to follow an 824 
exponential decline, with faster loss earlier in the disease.  (Tee et al., 2017b, Cabral et al., 825 
2017) although rates of reduction may vary (Robson et al., 2008a). XL forms of the disease 826 
are usually more severe, with early involvement of the fovea and more rapid decline of vision 827 
(Figure 7B).(Jayasundera et al., 2010) 828 
 On OCT imaging the disruption of the photoreceptor layer (EZ) starts from the 829 
periphery (rod rich region) and gradually the functioning retina constricts to the fovea, until 830 
it disappears, leading to blindness. Both the width of the residual EZ, as well as the area of 831 
EZ on en face analysis, are established sensitive measure s of RP progression.(Tee et al., 832 
2017a, Birch et al., 2013a) Several studies employing AOSLO have reported a decrease in 833 
cone density and/or increased cone spacing in patients with RP.(Georgiou et al., 2018)  834 
 Sector RP is an uncommon, restricted form of RP in which only one or two retinal 835 
quadrants display clinical pathological signs ( Figure 7C), and as such is associated with a 836 
good prognosis ; and associated with AR, AD and XL inheritance .(Coussa et al., 2019, 837 
Georgiou et al., 2020b)   838 
 839 
4.1.3. General Management of Rod-Cone Dystrophies 840 
Management of most forms of RCD is symptomatic. The two most common vision limiting 841 
complications (other than progression of retinal degeneration) are: i) cataract, and ii) cystoid 842 
macular oedema (CMO).  843 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
39 
Cataract surgery in RP -affected eyes has been reported to be associated  with a  844 
higher incidence of intraoperative complications.(Khojasteh et al., 2023) Visual acuity gains 845 
can be limited by retinal degeneration, and the removal of the physiologic photoprotection 846 
provided by the crystalline lens may theoretically accelerate retinal degeneration.(Khojasteh 847 
et al., 2023, Nguyen et al., 2023b)  Only a few studies have been published evaluating the 848 
visual outcomes in these patients post-surgery, and these studies are limited because they 849 
are n oncomparative and include eyes with coexisting ocular comorbidities such as 850 
pseudoexfoliation and epiretinal membrane.(Nguyen et al., 2023b, Jackson et al., 2001)  In 851 
a comparative study cataract surgery was found to be safe, but with higher rate of post -852 
operative CMO.(Georgiou, 2024)  853 
CMO is most prevalent in patients with AD inheritance (71.4% with CMO in at least 854 
one eye), followed by AR/sporadic inheritance (58.9%) and least common in XL inheritance 855 
(12.5%).(Liew et al., 2019)  Treatment approaches include: topical carbonic anhydrase 856 
inhibitors (CAIs), oral CAIs, periocular and intravitreal steroids, and intravitreal anti -VEGF 857 
agents.(Strong et al., 2017)  There is no level 1 evidence supporting topical/oral CAIs use 858 
and reports have demonstrated highly variable efficacy. In a 12 -month retrospective study, 859 
with primary endpoint the reduction of central macular thickness on OCT of at least 11% 860 
between visits: 53.1% of patients following treatment with topical dorzolamide and 41.2% of 861 
patients following treatment with oral acetazolamide demonstrated response. (Liew et al., 862 
2015) A prospective exploratory study demonstrated both the safety and acceptability of 863 
serial intravitreal aflibercept in patients with RP-CMO, with 37.9% of patients responding to 864 
treatment by 12 months. All patients demonstrating anatomical response did so after their 865 
first injection.(Strong et al., 2020) Beyond cataract and CMO management, orientation and 866 
mobility training combined with low -vision aids such as night -vision goggles, flashlights, 867 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
40 
and/or reverse telescopes, and assistive technologies can optimise residual visual function 868 
and increase patients’ independence.(Verbakel et al., 2018) 869 
 870 
4.1.4. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 871 
Two neurotrophic, pharmacological approaches have been explored that would potentially 872 
be relevant for all genetic forms of RCD. A ciliary neurot rophic factor ( CNTF)-releasing 873 
encapsulated RPE cell implant (NCT01530659) has been investigated in a phase II/III trial, 874 
with high dose or low -dose implant. Neither dose showed therapeutic benefit —with some 875 
patients experiencing loss of retinal sensitivity that was reversible on removal of the 876 
implant.(Birch et al., 2013b) However, a pilot study using AO imaging to investigate in vivo 877 
cone structure in three patients with CNTF implants over a 24 month period found that cone 878 
density remained stable in eyes with a CNTF implant , whereas there was continued cone 879 
loss in untreated fellow eyes.(Talcott et al., 2011)  880 
A phase I trial (NCT03063021) assessed the safety and tolerability of N -881 
acetylcysteine (NAC) in patients with RP. NAC was well-tolerated and mean BCVA 882 
significantly improved at 0.4, 0.5, and 0. 2 letters/month in the 600 mg (cohort 1), 1200 mg 883 
(cohort 2), and 1800 mg (cohort 3) cohorts respectively. There was no significant 884 
improvement in mean retinal sensitivity over time in cohorts 1 and 2, but there was in cohort 885 
3 (0.15 dB/month). There was n o significant change in mean EZ width in any 886 
cohort.(Campochiaro et al., 2020)  A randomi sed, placebo -controlled phase III  trial 887 
(NCT05537220) is on-going to determine if oral NAC can provide long -term stabilisation 888 
and/or improvement in visual function. 889 
 Rod-derived cone viability factor (RdCVF) is an inactive thioredoxin secreted by rod 890 
photoreceptors that protects cones from degeneration by stimulating aerobic glycolysis  in 891 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
41 
RP. The administration of RdCVF is a potential agnostic treatment for RP,(Aït-Ali et al., 892 
2015) with planned commencement of clinical trials. 893 
PIONEER phase I/II clinical trial is a first -in-human, multi -centre, open -label dose 894 
escalation clinical trial evaluating the safety and tolerability of GS030, an optogenetic 895 
treatment candidate combining an AAV2 -based gene therapy (GS030 -DP) with the use of 896 
light-stimulating goggles (GS030 -MD) in patients with end -stage RP. This therapeutic 897 
approach is independent of the causal mutation and therefore applicable to potentially all 898 
patients suffering from end -stage RP. Nine patients were recruited, with good safety  and 899 
tolerability for at least 1 year after injection at all tested doses, and encouraging efficacy 900 
signals observed in some patients. (Sahel et al., 2021)  Currently there is on -going 901 
recruitment of the GS030 extension cohort at the highest dose of 5e11 vg per eye. 902 
A phase IIb randomised, sham-controlled, multi-dose, multi-centre, double-masked 903 
clinical trial (RESTORE trial, NCT04945772) is evaluating the efficacy and safety of MCO -904 
010 optogenetic therapy in adults with RP. MCO -010 is an ambient -light activatable MCO 905 
optogenetic therapy  for vision restoration. Peer -reviewed results are anticipated. MCO 906 
approach is described in further detail under the STGD1 section. 907 
There is currently no proven specific gene guided treatment for RCD, but there are 908 
many on-going trials; prioritised genotypes are discussed below including MERTK, MYO7A, 909 
PDE6B, , RHO, RLBP1, RPGR, RP2, and USH2A. 910 
 911 
4.1.5. Electrophysiology 912 
The f fERGs in RP show wide variability but in most mild -moderate cases, ERGs show 913 
evidence of a rod-cone dystrophy (Robson et al., 2018). Scotopic strong flash ERG a-waves 914 
are reduced, localising dysfunction to the outer retina and rod photoreceptors, and there are 915 
milder photopic ER G abnormalities. ERGs can be grossly abnormal in the presence of 916 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
42 
normal or near -normal fundi. Ther e is t ypically ERG amplitude reductions and delays, in 917 
keeping with generalised retinal dysfunction, with delays often most obvious in the light-918 
adapted 30Hz flicker ERGs. Amplitude reduction alone can occur in cases of sector RP, in 919 
keeping with a localised loss of function, but generalised dysfunction can also occur in the 920 
presence of restricted intra-retinal pigment. In severe cases, rod and cone-mediated ERGs 921 
may be undetectable. Pattern ERG and mfERGs can show varying degrees of macular 922 
involvement (Robson et al., 2003; 2004; 2006).   923 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
43 
4.2. RPGR-RP 924 
4.2.1. Molecular Genetics 925 
RPGR constitutes the most prevalent monogenic cause of XL RP.(Georgiou et al., 2021b). 926 
The detail ed structure, function, and phenotype -genotype associations  for the gene are 927 
presented in the XL RPGR-COD/CORD section. 928 
 929 
4.2.2. Clinical Assessment 930 
X-linked RP is one of the most severe forms of RCD , accounting for approximately 20% of 931 
cases.(Tee et al., 2016a) The most common cause is RPGR gene variants (RPGR, OMIM 932 
312610) accounting for around 80% of the XL-RP, and several preclinical and clinical gene 933 
augmentation trials focus on the disease. (Awadh Hashem et al., 2023)  Along with typical 934 
symptoms and findings of RP, RPGR patients typically have high myopia.(Williams et al., 935 
2022) RPGR-disease can present as sectoral-RP and the phenotype in female carriers can 936 
be variable, ranging from asymptomatic to severe  RP.(Kalitzeos et a l., 2017, Georgiou et 937 
al., 2021a) Tapetal like reflex (TLR) is a clinical feature of the disease that can be present 938 
both in affected males and carriers. In a study of retinal sensitivity using Octopus 900 static 939 
perimetry, functional decline was evident with increasing age  and an overall annual 940 
exponential decline was observed. (Tee et al., 2018) Microperimetry assessment of RPGR 941 
patients showed that m ost patients lose retinal sensitivity rapidly during their second and 942 
third decades of life.  (Anikina et al., 2022)  Median VA falls below 20/200 in the fifth 943 
decade.(De Silva et al., 2023)  944 
 945 
4.2.3. Imaging 946 
EZ width (EZW) and ONL and inner retinal layer thickness measurements  on OCT have 947 
been investigated for RPGR-RP and show  good repeatability and high inter -ocular 948 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
44 
symmetry.(Tee et al., 2017a)   Similarly, analysis of en-face OCT imaging of the EZ area 949 
(EZA) from high-density macular volume scans have been quantified, showing decreasing 950 
EZA with age and high symmetry between eyes .(Tee et al., 2019)  A common FAF finding 951 
of the disease is  the presence of peri -foveal rings of increase d signal. Baseline ring area 952 
and constriction rate correlate negatively with age, and constriction rate correlates strongly 953 
with baseline area, with age, but not genotype, exert ing a significant effect on constriction 954 
rates, with greatest rates of progression seen in younger subjects. (Tee et al., 2017b) 955 
 956 
4.2.4. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 957 
Successful AAV gene augmentation has been performed in murine and canine models, with 958 
preservation of photoreceptor nuclei and inner/outer segments limited to treated 959 
areas.(Pawlyk et al., 2016, Beltran et al., 2012) The arrest of disease progression was also 960

or abbreviation of the repetitive sequence.(Pawlyk et al., 2016) 966 
Three phase I/II clinical trials (NCT03316560, NCT03252847 and NCT03116113) 967

4.10.1. Molecular Genetics 1182 
Enhanced S -Cone Syndrome ( NR2E3, OMIM 604485) is a rare AR form of retinal 1183 
degeneration, caused by variants in the nuclear receptor NR2E3.(Audo et al., 2008, de 1184

4.10.2. Clinical Assessment 1189 
ESCS is a slowly progressive retinal degeneration . Patients report early onset night 1190 
blindness. Fundus findings are typically characterised by nummular pigment clumping at the 1191 
level of the RPE, often most plentiful around the temporal vascular arcades (Figure 8).(Audo 1192 
et al., 2008, de Carvalho et al., 2020) Other imaging findings include: torpedo-like changes, 1193 
deep atrophic lesions with a small hyperpigmented rim, helicoid subretinal fibrosis, 1194

around the optic nerve head, and yellow-white dots in areas of relatively normal -appearing 1196 
retina.(Yzer et al., 2013, Khan et al., 2010) 1197 
 1198 
4.10.3. Imaging 1199 
OCT studies may show a disturbed perimacular region (thought to be filled with S -cone 1200 
photoreceptors instead of rods), with thick and bulgin g retina and abnormal laminar 1201 
architecture (Figure 8).(Jacobson et al., 2004, de Carvalho et al., 2020)  1202
