4.10.4. Electrophysiology 1204 
The ERGs are pathognomonic in almost all cases with a highly distinctive pattern of 1205 
abnormality (Audo et al., 2008, de Carvalho et al., 2021) . Dim flash rod ERGs are 1206 
undetectable, in keeping with an absence of rods. Scotopic strong flash ERGs and photopic 1207 
single flash cone ERGs are dominated by short-wavelength-sensitive mechanisms, leading 1208 
to a similar simplified and severely delayed waveforms. The photopic 30Hz flicker ERG is 1209 
smaller than the single flash cone ERG, in keeping with lower S -cone system temporal 1210 
resolution. S-cone ERGs are of higher amplitude compared to single flash cone ERGs than 1211 
in normal subjects, consistent with the increased expression of S-cone opsin. The absolute 1212 
amplitudes of responses can show wide variability and be grossly attenuated, but the rate 1213 
of age-associated reduction in ERGs is similar to that seen in health, suggesting relatively 1214 
stable peripheral dysfunction  (de Carvalho et al., 2021) . Pattern ERGs can be delayed 1215 
and/or reduced, and show worsening with progressive maculopathy. 1216 
 1217 
4.10.5. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 1218 
NR2E3 may serve as a genetic modifier and therapeutic agent to potentially treat a range of 1219 
retinal degenerative diseases. Nr2e3 was administered in five unique mouse models of RP, 1220 
and resulted in reduced retinal degeneration as observed by increase in photoreceptor cells, 1221 
improved electroretinogram, and a molecular reset of key transcription factors and 1222 
associated gene networks. (Li et al., 2020)  In patient-derived iPSCs, correction of NR2E3 1223 
associated ESCS has been described using CRISPR -Cas9.(Bohrer et al., 2019)  A phase 1224 
I/II multi-centre gene therapy study (NCT05203939) aims to assess the safety and efficacy 1225 
of Ocu400 (AAV5-NR2E3).  1226

5. LEBER CONGENITAL AMAUROSIS/EARLY-ONSET SEVERE RETINAL 1253 
DYSTROPHY (LCA/EOSRD) 1254 
LCA and early -onset severe retinal dystrophy (EOSRD) are characterised by severe 1255 
congenital/early-onset visual loss, nystagmus, amaurotic pupils  and markedly 1256 
reduced/absent ffERGs.(Kumaran et al., 2017). Below we discuss genotypes with treatment 1257 
avenues being explored . LCA/EOSRD are both genetically and phenotypically 1258 
heterogeneous. The identified genes account for approximately 70%–80%. CEP290, CRB1, 1259 
GUCY2D, RDH12 and RPE65 are the most common  and their imaging findings are 1260 
presented below, together with AIPL1, NMNAT1 and TULP1 due to distinctive findings are 1261 
also presented. 1262 
 1263 
General Management of LCA/EOSRD 1264 
The severity of visual impairment and rate of visual loss varies considerably both within and 1265 
between genotypes. The current management for most of the patients with LCA/EOSRD 1266 
remains symptomatic, with treatment of possible associated complications (lens 1267 
opacification, vitreoretinal interphase abnormalities, CMO), and lifestyle recommendations 1268 
including a healthy diet, and also supportive, in terms of optimising function for education 1269 
and the work place with optical aids and assistive technologies.(Daich Varela et al., 2022a) 1270 
Renal evaluation is recommended  in genotypes that can be associated with 1271 
nephronophthisis/renal failure, such as CEP290 and IQCB1. Vitamin A, minerals and amino 1272 
acids supplementation have failed to show a clear benefit for these patients, and are 1273 
consequently not recommended. Patients are often advised to avoid excessive exposure to 1274 
bright sunlight, including wearing good u ltraviolet light-blocking sunglasses . Smoke and 1275 
tobacco free environments are also suggested for these patients, as there is evidence 1276 
suggesting that smoking may further increase damage to the retina - extrapolating from age-1277 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
62 
related macular degeneration. Refractive error should be corrected, use of low vision aids 1278 
when possible, and optimal access to educational and work -related opportunities. Infants 1279 
with severe visual impairment may also have delays or difficulties with speech, social skills, 1280 
and behaviour, highlighting the importance of a multi-disciplinary approach - with review by 1281 
a specialist developmental paediatrician critical to help mitigate additional challeng es in 1282 
severely visually impaired children.  1283 
Journal Pre-proof

5.1.1. Molecular Genetics 1285 
RPE65 functions as an isomerohydrolase and plays a key role in the retinoid cycle of 1286 
vision (recycling the chromophore following photoisomerisation). Bi-allelic variants give 1287 
rise to LCA/EOSRD.  1288

5.1.2. Clinical Assessment 1290 
Among LCA/EOSRD, RPE65 causes about 5% -10% of cases, and most children with 1291 
RPE65-associated retinopathy present with severe night blindness from birth. (Kumaran et 1292 
al., 2017) Findings are typical of LCA as mentioned above. Patients with RPE65- retinopathy 1293 
can also present with severe early childhood onset retinal dystrophy (SECORD)/EOSRD, a 1294 
phenotype with a greater degree of visual function compare to LCA, reflected both in better 1295 
VA and ERGs (Weleber et al., 2011). 1296 
 1297 
5.1.3. Imaging 1298 
RPE65-deficiency (OMIM 180069) is associated with reduced or absent AF on FAF imaging, 1299 
suggesting low or absent levels of lipofuscin in the RPE (Figure 10A).(Lorenz et al., 2004, 1300 
Kumaran et al., 2020) OCT studies have demonstrated relatively normal retinal thickness in 1301 
some patients; with more commonly  a central macular area of relatively preserved retina 1302 
with a surrounding ring of thinning or more widespread retinal loss (Figure 10A).(Jacobson 1303 
et al., 2005, Kumaran et al., 2020)  1304 
 1305 
5.1.4. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 1306

therapeutic efforts given the gene size, the severity of the disease and the relative 1308

AAV), Spark Therap eutics), and an on -going phase I/II trial which has reported promising 1311 
efficacy (NCT02946879, AAV5-OPTIRPE65). VN-AAV achieved an improvement in  multi-1312 
luminance mobility test (MLMT) at 4 years and maintained an average improvement in FST 1313 
testing, reflecting more than a 2 log 10(cd.s/m2) improvement in light sensitivity .(Maguire et 1314 
al., 2019) Improved rod-mediated function has been shown with two-colour dark-adapted 1315 
perimetry (2cDAP) in patients with RPE65-retinal dystrophy treated with VN.(Ku et al., 2023)  1316 
The wide availability of approved treatment for LCA-RPE65 has further improved our 1317 
understanding of the  potential challenges of gene therapy for IRD ranging from logistical 1318 
and financial, to how to appropriately train and disseminate the most appropriate surgical 1319 
delivery, to perifoveal chorioretinal atrophy and sympathetic ophthalmia.(Stingl et al., 2023, 1320 
Lidder et al., 2023)  A subset of patients undergoing subretinal VN injection develop 1321 
progressive perifoveal chorioretinal atrophy after surgery. It is yet unclear what ocular, 1322 
surgical delivery, and vector -related factors predis pose to this complication. (Gange et al., 1323 
2022) Significant national and international discussions are on -going around all of these 1324

5.3. CEP290 - LCA/EOSRD 1353 
 1354 
5.3.1. Molecular Genetics 1355 
Of the reported CEP290 gene variants, the c.2991+1655A>G intronic variant is the most 1356 
common disease-causing variant, having been identified in at least one allele in 58 -77% of 1357 
CEP290-LCA patients.(Sheck et al., 2018, McAnany et al., 2013) The aforementioned deep-1358 
intronic variant results in a pseudo -exon inclusion in half of the RNA in blood cells and 1359 
lymphoblasts, which is significantly increased in patient-derived retinal organoids, explaining 1360 
why this variant is associated with non-syndromic LCA instead of Joubert syndrome.(Parfitt 1361 
et al., 2016) 1362 
 1363 
5.3.2. Clinical Assessment - Imaging 1364 
OCT studies of CEP290-LCA/EOSRD (OMIM 610142) have shown that despite profound 1365 
cone dysfunction, the foveal architecture is structurally preserved until the fourth decade of 1366 
life in some patients , although with abnormal inner and outer segments , in contrast to the 1367 
early loss of rod photoreceptors. (Sheck et al., 2018, Pasadhika et al., 2010)  FAF imaging 1368 
reveals a perifoveal hyperautofluorescent ring in most patients  (Figure 10C), and areas of 1369 
decreased signal in older patients (pigmentary retinopathy).(Sheck et al., 2018)  1370 
 1371 
5.3.3. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 1372 
Phase I/II (AGN-151587 (EDIT-101), NCT03872479) and phase II/III (sepofarsen (QR-110), 1373 
NCT03913143), a gene editing and an AON-mediated exon skipping trial , respectively, 1374 
recruited patients with compound heterozygous or homozygous intron 26 variant  1375 
c.2991+1655A>G in CEP290. EDIT-101 removes the aberrant splice donor created by the 1376 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
68 
variant in the CEP290 gene and restores normal CEP290 expression.(Maeder et al., 2019) 1377 
Promising early results have been presented for EDIT-101, but the trial is currently paused 1378 
awaiting further funding for an expansion phase.  1379 
The phase II/III AON trial did not meet its endpoints, but a further phase III trial is 1380 
anticipated.  In a case report of the long term outcomes of a patient treated with AON; all 1381 
visual function measures and IS/OS intensity on the OCT showed transient improvements, 1382 
peaking at 3-6 months, remaining better than baseline at ∼2 years, and returning to baseline 1383 
by 3-4 years after each single injection.(Cideciyan et al., 2023)  1384

5.5. RDH12 - LCA/EOSRD 1412 
5.5.1. Molecular Genetics 1413 
Retinol dehydrogenase 12 (RDH12) is a small gene located on chromosome 14, encoding 1414 
an enzyme capable of metaboli sing retinoids. It is primarily located in photoreceptor inner 1415 
segments and thereby is believed to have an important role in clearing excessive retinal and 1416 
other toxic aldehydes produced by light exposure. (Daich Varela and Michaelides, 2022)  1417 
Heterozygous variant  RDH12 c.763delG is associated with a n AD  RCD with variable 1418 
expression, with a gain of function mechan ism.(Muthiah et al., 2022)  Hypomorphic alleles 1419 
of this gene cause relatively mild retinopathy with predominant macular involvement.  (Ba-1420 
Abbad et al., 2020b) 1421

5.5.2. Clinical Assessment 1423 
RDH12-associated disease  (OMIM 608830), often gives rise to an EOSRD phenotype 1424 
characterised by early -dense intraretinal pigment migration and maculopathy. (Schuster et 1425 
al., 2007, Fahim et al., 2019) 1426 
 1427 
5.5.3. Imaging 1428 
OCT reveal s severe loss of structure often from 10 years of  age.(Fahim et al., 2019)  1429 
Peripapillary sparing and macular atrophy are common findings on FAF (centrally decreased 1430 
signal), and with disease progression, the area of atrophy extends peripherally in a 1431 
variegated watercolour-like pattern (Figure 11B), which usually corresponds to the retinal 1432 
vasculature.(Fahim et al., 201 9) Recently the phenotypic spectrum of RDH12 has been 1433 
extended to include later onset and milder phenotypes.(Scott et al., 2020, Ba-Abbad et al., 1434 
2020a, Muthiah et al., 2022, Daich Varela et al., 2023e)  1435

5.6.1. Clinical Assessment 1443 
TULP1 (OMIM 602280), AIPL1 (OMIM 604323) and NMNAT1 (OMIM 608700) -associated 1444 
disease are characterised by early maculopathy and profound visual impairment from birth. 1445 
NMNAT1 maculopathy typically is severe, early onset and extensive ( Figure 11C), with 1446 
pigment clumping (including nummular pigmentation), both visible on FAF and OCT.(Falk et 1447 
al., 2012) Similar to RDH12, the phenotypic spectrum of NMNAT1 has been extended to 1448 
later onset and a milder phenotype. (Kumaran e t al., 2018)  In AIPL1-LCA no patient is 1449

et al., 2010, Aboshiha et al., 2015)  1451 
 1452 
5.6.2. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 1453 
AIPL1 – LCA (AILP1, OMIM 604392) is a rare cause of LCA (1-2% of the cases).(Sacristan-1454 
Reviriego et al., 2018, Sacristan-Reviriego et al., 2020) The window of opportunity is narrow 1455 
given there is no residual outer retinal structure after the age of 4 years. (Pasadhika et al., 1456 
2010, Aboshiha et al., 2015)  A compassionate use gene therapy study  (MHRA Specials 1457 
Licence) is on-going for AIPL1-LCA, aiming to rescue function and preserve structure in 1458 
infants and young children with residual structure , with safety and benefit reported out to 3 1459 
years post-surgery.  1460

6. CONE DYSFUNCTION SYNDROMES 1461 
The cone dysfunction syndromes (CDS) are stationary cone disorders, with congenital/early-1462 
infantile onset an d give rise to purely cone dysfunction, whereas the aforementioned 1463 
progressive cone dystrophies are of later -onset and usually also involve rod loss over 1464 
time.(Aboshiha et al., 2016) Cone dysfunction syndromes are rarer than COD/CORD, with 1465 
10 genes  implicated and five distinct phenotypes described to date; (complete and 1466 
incomplete) achromatopsia (ACHM), blue -cone monochromatism (BCM), Bornholm eye 1467 
disease (BED), oligocone trichromacy (OT)  and RGS9/R9AP-retinopathy/bradyopsia; all 1468 
described below.  1469 
 1470 
6.1. Achromatopsia 1471 
ACHM is an AR disorder with a heterogeneous genetic background. ACHM is the most 1472 
common CDS affecting 1 in 30,000.(Michaelides et al., 2004c) 1473 
 1474 
6.1.1. Molecular Genetics  1475 
Disease-causing variants have been reported in  ATF6 (Kohl et al., 2015) (OMIM 605537), 1476 
CNGA3 (Wissinger et al., 1998, Wissinger et al., 2001)  (ACHM2, OMIM600053), CNGB3 1477 
(Mayer et al., 2017, Johnson et al., 2004)  (ACHM3, OMIM605080), GNAT2 (Kohl et al., 1478 
2002, Aligianis et al., 2002)  (ACHM4, OMIM139340) , PDE6C (Aboshiha et al ., 2016)  1479 
(OMIM600827) and PDE6H (OMIM 601190) (Kohl et al., 2012) , which all encode crucial 1480 
components of the cone-specific phototransduction cascade, with the exception of ATF6.  1481 
 1482 
6.1.2. Clinical Assessment 1483 
The condition presents either at birth or early infancy, with poor VA, pendular nystagmus, 1484 
marked photophobia, and colour vision loss along all three axes.(Hirji et al., 2018a) In depth 1485 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
75 
investigations of molecularly confirm ed cohorts with variants in individual gene s have 1486 
highlighted distinct phenotypic features for the different genotypes in terms of 1487 
electrophysiology and retinal structure. 1488 
 1489 
6.1.3. Imaging 1490 
Four distinct FAF phenotypes have been reported: i. normal appearance, ii.  central 1491 
increased signal, iii. reduced signal centrally, (Aboshiha et al., 2014) and iv. central area of 1492 
decreased signal, with a surrounding ring of hyperautofluorescence  (Figure 12 A -1493 
D).(Georgiou et al., 2019c)   OCT imaging can be used to grade ACHM into five grades: I. 1494 
continuous EZ, II. EZ disruption, III. EZ absence, IV. presence of a hyporeflective zone, and 1495 
V. outer retinal atrophy with RPE loss (Figure 12 E-I).(Sundaram et al., 2014b)  1496 
 1497 
OCT findings in a large cohort of ACHM patients (n=50), with a proportional incidence-based 1498 
representation of all genotypes, and substantial follow -up, support that the condition is 1499 
predominantly stable in the vast majority of patients. (Hirji et al., 2018b) Foveal hypoplasia, 1500 
preservation of inner retinal layers over the fovea, is a common OCT finding in ACHM 1501 
(Langlo et al., 2016b, Genead et al., 2011a, Sundaram et al., 2014a, Thiadens et al., 2010, 1502 
Thomas et al., 2011).  1503 
 1504 
ACHM is arguably the most well studied condition using AOSLO imaging. (Georgiou et al., 1505 
2018) Early investigations with confocal AOSLO identified ‘dark spaces’ in the cone mosaic, 1506 
increased cone spacing, and decreased cone density ( Figure 13 1A).(Carroll et al., 2008, 1507 
Genead et al., 2011b, Dubis et al., 2014, Merino et al., 2011) Marked variability in the cone 1508 
mosaic has been observed across patients  (Figure 13). Cone densities can be evaluated 1509 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
76 
with good repeatability in ACHM, (Georgiou et al., 2020c)  and OCT art efacts can have a 1510 
predictive value for successful AOSLO imaging.(Litts et al., 2021) 1511 
 1512 
6.1.4. Management  1513 
Current management includes correct diagnosis, in order to offer accurate prognosis and 1514 
genetic counselling, as well as refractive correction, low visual aids, and educational 1515 
support. Deep red tinted lenses can help with disabling photophobia, by reducing rod 1516 
saturation.(Schornack et al., 2007)  There is on -going research and therapeutic trials in 1517 
different genotypes, which are presented for each genotype in the next sections.  1518 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
77 
6.2. CNGA3- and CNGB3-associated Achromatopsia 1519 
 1520 
6.2.1. Molecular Genetics  1521 
CNGA3 and CNGB3 encode the α-subunit and β-subunit of the cGMP -gated cationic 1522 
channel respectively;  (Mayer et al., 2017, Fesenko et al., 1985)  located in cone outer 1523 
segments, categorising CNGA3/CNGB3-associated ACHM as a “channelopathy”.(Yu et al., 1524 
1996) It has been proven in vitro  that α -subunits can create functional channels in the 1525 
absence of β-subunits, in direct contrast to β-subunits.(Wissinger et al., 1997)  CNGA3 1526 
variants have greater allelic heterogeneity compared to CNGB3.(Michaelides et al., 2004c, 1527 
Johnson et al., 2004) The p.(Arg403Gln) variant in CNGB3, located in the middle of the pore 1528 
domain, when associated with the nonsense variant p.(Thr383fs), has been reported to 1529 
result in progressive cone dystrophy (Michaelides et a l., 2004a) . Moreover, digenic or 1530 
triallelic inheritance, involving p.(Arg403Gln) has also been reported - often associated with 1531 
a phenotype distinct from typical ACHM (Burkard et al., 2018). 1532 
 1533 
6.2.2. Imaging 1534 
FAF patterns in CNGA3- and CNGB3-ACHM include: i. normal appearance, ii. central 1535 
increased signal, iii. reduced signal centrally. (Aboshiha et al., 2014)  All OCT grades have 1536 
been observed (with approximately 50% of cases being grade I to III); suggesting residual 1537 
cones in a significant proportion of patients. The presence of residu al cones is critical for 1538 
targeting by gene therapy intervention.(Langlo et al., 2016a, Georgiou et al., 2019b)  Foveal 1539 
hypoplasia is present in 60-70% of previously reported patients with CNGA3- and CNGB3-1540 
ACHM.(Langlo et al., 2016b, Genead et al., 2011a, Sundaram et al., 2014a, Thiadens et al., 1541 
2010, Thomas et al., 2011) Marked variability in the cone mosaic has been observed across 1542 
patients with AOSLO; with no significant difference between CNGA3 and CNGB3.(Genead 1543 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
78 
et al., 2011b, Sundaram et al., 2014b, Litts et al., 2020) In a large cohort of CNGB3-ACHM, 1544 
significantly decreased peak foveal cone densities and increased spacing has been 1545 
reported.(Langlo et al., 2016a)  Similar findings were also reported for CNGA3-ACHM 1546 
(n=38), also including a high degree of interocular symmetry, and vast intrafamilial 1547 
variability.(Georgiou et al., 2019b)  1548 
 1549 
6.2.3. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 1550 
In CNGB3-ACHM, gene supplementation in a canine model showed improved cone function 1551 
and daylight vision.(Komaromy et al., 2010) Currently there are two on-going phase I/II gene 1552 
therapy trials (NCT03001310 and NCT02599922). A canine study which  explored ciliary 1553 
neurotrophic factor (CNTF) effects on cone photoreceptors had promising 1554 
efficacy.(Komaromy et al., 201 3) However, in humans, no measurably enhanced cone 1555 
function was observed , which may partly be due to a species difference between human 1556 
and canine CNGB3 cone response to CNTF (NCT01648452).(Zein et al., 2014) The results 1557 
of the first in human  clinical trial were recently published, demonstrating an acceptable 1558 
safety and tolerability profile, and improvements in several efficacy parameters indicating 1559 
that AAV8-hCARp.hCNGB3 gene therapy may provide benefit.(Michaelides et al., 2023) 1560 
 In CNGA3-ACHM, gene supplementation in a knock -out CNGA3 mouse model has 1561 
shown restoration of cone -specific visual processing in the central nervous 1562 
system.(Michalakis et al., 2010)  Further investigation showed greater therapeutic benefit 1563 
(improvement of ERG amplitude) in younger mice, suggesting that age of treatment and the 1564 
extent of photoreceptor degeneration may affect outcome. (Muhlfriedel et al., 2017)  In a 1565 
naturally occurring CNGA3 mouse model, gene replacement resulted in restoration of cone 1566 
ERG responses, improvement of VA and contrast sensitivity, and halted cone 1567 
degeneration.(Pang et al., 2012) In a sheep model, similar intervention led to an improved 1568 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
79 
cone ERG, with a sustained effect.(Gootwine et al., 2017) Subretinal gene therapy in adults 1569 
with CNGA3-ACHM, with AAV8.CNGA3 ( NCT02610582) was not associated with 1570 
substantial safety problems and was associated with cone photoreceptor activation in adult 1571 
patients, as reflected by VA and contrast sensitivity gains.(Fischer et al., 2020) The results 1572 
of the three year follow -up of the same study demonstrated a good safety profile of the 1573 
therapy even at the highest dose administered, with the small sample size limiting the 1574 
statistical power of efficacy analyses, with amblyopia in adult patients as a potential limiting 1575 
factor. (Reichel et al., 2022)  1576 
Combining functional MRI population receptive field mapping and psychophysics with 1577 
stimuli that selectively measure cone photoreceptor signalling, the cortical and visual cone 1578 
function before and after gene therapy in four paediatric patients with CNGA3- and CNGB3-1579 
ACHM was investigated. After treatment, two of the four children displayed strong evidence 1580 
for novel cone-mediated signals in the visual cortex, with a retinotopic pattern that was not 1581 
present in untreated ACHM. Importantly, this change was paired  with a significant 1582 
improvement in psychophysical measures of cone -mediated visual function (Farahbakhsh 1583 
et al., 2022) . Currently there are three on -going phase I/II gene therapy trials , including 1584 
paediatric participants (NCT03758404 and NCT02935517).   1585

6.6. Blue Cone Monochromatism (BCM) 1649 
 1650 
6.6.1. Molecular Genetics 1651 
BCM is an X-linked condition, with variants within the opsin array resulting in inactivation of 1652 
the array and absent / markedly reduced L -opsin and M-opsin expression, (OMIM 300821, 1653 
300822, 300824).  The genetic mechanisms can be broadly divided into 3 groups: (i) 1654 
deletions confined to the locus control region (LCR), (ii) OPN1LW (encodes L-cone opsin) 1655 
and OPN1MW (encodes M-cone opsin)-related variants, where in a 2-step process, often a 1656 
single L-/M-hybrid opsin gene is inactivated by a missense variant (p.(Cys203Arg) being the 1657 
commonest), and (iii) specific combinations of single nucleotide polymorphisms in exon 3 of 1658 
L-/M- opsins ('L/M interchange haplotypes'), which cause aberrant splicing (LIAVA being 1659 
one of the most common). (Gardner et al., 2009)  Often the most severe phenotype is 1660 
associated with deletions involving the LCR; although there is considerable intra- and inter-1661 
genotype group variability.(Gardner et al., 2014) Female carriers are asymptomatic. 1662 
 1663 
6.6.2. Clinical Assessment 1664 
Fundus examination usually reveals a myopic but otherwise normal retina.(Aboshiha et al., 1665 
2016, Gardner et al., 2009) The presenting symptoms can be similar to ACHM, including 1666 
photophobia, nystagmus, and decreased VA.(Michaelides et al., 2005d)  1667 
 1668 
6.6.3. Imaging 1669 
OCT and FAF imaging can be similar to ACHM, with a variable degree of EZ 1670 
disruption.(Gardner et al., 2014)  In AOSLO imaging, cone mosaic disruption is highly 1671 
variable and may be genotype specific (Carroll et al., 2012, Patterson et al., 2022). The cone 1672 
mosaic appears dark over th e foveal centre with confocal AOSLO, with a sparse array of 1673 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
84 
large bright spots, which are believed to be S-cones, surrounding it.(Patterson, 2017, Carroll 1674 
et al., 2012) Cone density is higher than that expected for the S-cone sub-mosaic, in keeping 1675 
with remnant L -/M-cones.(Cideciyan et al., 2013, Curcio et al., 1990, Curcio et al., 1991)  1676 
Despite female carriers being asymptomatic, co nfocal AOSLO has demonstrated variably 1677 
reduced cone density, increased spacing, and disrupted organisation, with phenotypic 1678 
variability likely relating to random X-chromosome inactivation (Carroll et al., 2010). 1679 
 1680 
6.6.4. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 1681 
No proven treatment is available. Current management includes optimal correction of the 1682 
often-observed refractive error (high myopia) and tinted glasses/spectacles for 1683 
photoaversion (often with a magenta tint).  Exogenously expressed human opsins can 1684 
regenerate cone outer segments and rescue M -cone function in  Opn1mw-/-mice, thus 1685 
providing a proof-of-concept gene therapy in an animal model of BCM. (Zhang et al., 2017, 1686 
Deng et al., 2018)  1687 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
85 
6.7. Bornholm Eye Disease (BED) 1688 
 1689 
6.7.1. Molecular Genetics 1690 
In keeping with BCM, BED (OMIM 300821, 300822) is an X -linked cone dysfunction 1691 
syndrome also due to variants in the L/M opsin gene array.(Schwartz et al., 1990, Young et 1692 
al., 2004, Michaelides et al., 2005c, Guo et al., 2010, Li et al., 2015) Rare haplotypes (‘L/M 1693 
interchange haplotypes’) have been identified at polymorphic positions in exon 3 of the opsin 1694 
genes, that result in intermixing between L - and M -opsin genes, and is the principal 1695 
underlying genetic basis of  BED.(Gardner et al., 2014, McClements et al., 2013)  Some of 1696 
these interchange haplotypes have been shown to result in aberrant splicing of the opsin 1697 
genes and a variable degree of exon 3 skipping.(Gardner et al., 2014, Ueyama et al., 2012) 1698 
 1699 
6.7.2. Clinical Assessment 1700 
BED is an X -linked CDS associated with dichromacy and myopia, decreased VA, RPE 1701 
thinning, and visible choroidal vessels in the posterior pole. (Aboshiha et al., 2016, 1702 
Michaelides et al., 2005c) VA is impaired (often 20/40 to 20/80) from birth/early infancy, and 1703 
reduced cone responses are observed on ERG.(Haim et al., 1988, Schwartz et al., 1990, 1704 
Young et al., 2004, Michaelides et al., 2005c) 1705 
 1706 
6.7.3. Imaging 1707 
Retinal thinning can be observed on OCT. (Patterson et al., 2016)  AOSLO imaging shows 1708 
evidence that patients with BED have a significantly disrupted cone mosaic. (Patterson et 1709 
al., 2016) Split detection AOSLO reveals cone inner segments  in observed “ dark” spaces 1710 
with confocal AOSLO; these cells may be possible targets for gene therapy intervention 1711 
(Patterson et al., 2018) . The degree of photoreceptor mosai c disruption is highly variable, 1712 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
86 
with cone density ranging from near normal to more than 75% reduction; predominantly due 1713 
to the heterogeneity in the underlying genotype (predominantly L/M interchange 1714 
haplotypes).(Neitz et al., 2011)  Cone density has been found to correlate with both axial 1715 
length and the degree of myopia.(Patterson et al., 2016)  1716 
 1717 
6.7.4. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 1718 
Current management includes correct diagnosis, increasingly aided by molecular genet ic 1719 
testing, and optimal correction of the refractive error. Antisense oligonucleotide-mediated 1720 
exon skipping to abrogate some of these disease -causing variants is being therapeutically 1721 
explored for BED and BCM; and are currently in clinical trial for other  IRDs including LCA-1722 
CEP290 and USH2A-associated retinopathy.(Georgiou et al., 2021c)  1723 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
87 
6.8. Oligocone Trichromacy (OT) 1724 
 1725 
6.8.1. Molecular Genetics 1726 
The disease follows an autosomal recessive pattern of inheritance, no definite disease -1727 
causing gene(s) has been identified to date. Reports in the literature link similar phenotypes 1728 
to some of the ACHM causing genes (CNGA3, CNGB3, GNAT2 and PDE6C),(Aboshiha et 1729 
al., 2016) as well as CEP290.(Roosing et al., 2017) 1730 
 1731 
6.8.2. Clinical Assessment 1732 
The disease hallmark is the reduced amplitude of cone responses on ERG, with normal or 1733 
near-normal colour discrimination, and normal fundus appearance. (Aboshiha et al., 2016, 1734 
Andersen et al., 2010) The VA is usually mildly reduced (20/30 to 20/40). In contrast to other 1735 
CDS, there is mild/absent nystagmus and mild/absent photophobia. (Michaelides et al., 1736 
2004b, Andersen et al., 2010, Michaelides et al., 2011)  In some cases the disease can be 1737 
slowly progressive.(Michaelides et al., 2004b, Smirnov et al., 2015) 1738 
 1739 
6.8.3. Imaging 1740 
On OCT imaging the outer segment length was reduced, with decreased intensity of the EZ 1741 
outside the fovea.(Michaelides et al., 2011) The cone photoreceptor mosaic in OT has been 1742 
investigated with AO imaging, and in keeping with the original disease mechanism 1743 
hypothesis, a decreased number (‘oligocone’) of otherwise normal appearing foveal cones 1744 
(thereby permitting ‘trichromacy’) was obser ved, thereby confirming the disease 1745 
nomenclature (Michaelides et al., 2011). 1746 
 1747 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
88 
6.8.4. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 1748 
No proven treatment is available.  1749 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
89 
6.9. RGS9/R9AP-associated retinopathy ('Bradyopsia') 1750 
 1751 
6.9.1. Molecular Genetics 1752 
The disease -causing variants are in the RGS9 or R9AP genes, which encode proteins 1753 
responsible for rapid recovery of the visual cycle after a light stimulus.(Aboshiha et al., 2016) 1754 
RGS9 encodes a member of the regulators of the G protein signalling family of GTPase 1755 
activating proteins that function in various si gnalling pathways by accelerating the 1756 
deactivation of G proteins.(Cowan et al., 1998) RGS9 anchors to photoreceptor membranes 1757 
in retinal cells and deactivates G proteins in the rod and cone phototransduction cascades; 1758 
R9AP encodes for the respective anchoring protein.(Hu and Wensel, 2002) 1759 
 1760 
6.9.2. Clinical Assessment 1761 
RGS9/R9AP-associated retinopathy (OMIM 604067, 607814) presents from early childhood 1762 
with delayed dark and light adaption, (Nishiguchi et al., 2004)  which can be a common 1763 
feature also in OT, (Michaelides et al., 2011)  as well as other findings such as a normal 1764 
fundus appearance, reduced VA, and normal colour vision.(Michaelides et al., 2010b, 1765 
Hartong et al., 2007, Cheng et al., 2007, Khan, 2017)  RGS9/R9AP-associated retinopathy 1766 
is clinically indistinguishable from OT, but can be discerned using  non-standard extended 1767 
electrophysiological assessment or molecular genetic testing. ERG findings include normal 1768 
initial response in dark-adapted flicker ERGs performed with a dim stimulus (Michaelides et 1769 
al., 2010b, Thompson et al., 2018). 1770 
 1771 
6.9.3. Imaging 1772 
OCT and FAF are usually normal.(Khan, 2017) Cellular phenotyping is able to differentiate 1773 
between OT and RGS9/R9AP-associated retinopathy (Georgiou et al., 2018). Unlike in OT, 1774 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
90 
where the mosaic is disrupted, confocal AOSLO has revealed a normal cone photoreceptor 1775 
mosaic in subjects with RGS9/R9AP-associated retinopathy. (Strauss et al., 2015, 1776 
Michaelides et al., 2011) 1777 
 1778 
6.9.4. Therapeutics, Clinical Trials 1779 
No proven treatment is available.  1780 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
91 
7. CONCLUDING REMARKS & FUTURE PROSPECTS 1781 
 1782 
7.1. Establishing Genetic Diagnosis 1783 
Advances in molecular genetic techniques have greatly simplified molecular diagnosis and 1784 
transformed the field of retinal genetics. However, as with every advancement and its 1785 
promise (e.g., whole genome sequencing), new limitations and challenges are identified. To 1786 
date, even with the most developed pipelines and expertise, 20 -30% of patients remain 1787 
molecularly unsolved. 1788 
Increasing accessibility and broader testing are projected to improve the yield of 1789 
genetic investigations. The field has progressed from phenotype-guided single gene testing 1790 
to panel testing, encompassing a growing number of genes. Furthermore, advancements 1791 
have led to the adoption of whole exome sequencing and whole genome sequencing, 1792 
facilitating the resolution of previously unsolved cases. Alongside the broader coverage of 1793 
available tests, their cost has decreased, and accessibility has increased. This decreasing 1794 
cost has led to greater application of genetic testing in national healthcare systems and 1795 
insurance-based systems. Additionally, the availab ility of commercially available treatment 1796 
for RPE65 LCA/EOSRD and the numerous ongoing and upcoming trials incentivize industry 1797 
to make panel genetic testing available at no cost in many instances, aiming to identify 1798 
potential candidates for therapeutics a nd trials. Consequently, genetic testing is no longer 1799 
limited to tertiary referral centres, and we expect an increase in the number of patients being 1800 
tested in the future. Nevertheless, a large proportion of patients globally do not have access 1801 
to genetic testing. However, with ever decreasing costs, this will be addressed over the next 1802 
decade - for the benefit of these patients and their families, and the wider global IRD 1803 
community. 1804 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
92 
The large volume of data generated from widespread testing requires a syst ematic 1805 
approach and international standardized guidelines for evaluation. Patients tested in the 1806 
community should be provided access to genetic counsellors for result interpretation and 1807 
counselling. Interpretation of genetics results by physicians without experience or training in 1808 
IRD may lead to harmful delays in diagnosis, inaccurate prognostication, access to trials, or 1809 
therapeutics. Therefore, training the next generation of specialists should be at the epicentre 1810 
of the IRD community. The re is an  increasing need for clinical specialists, further 1811 
exacerbated by the demand for specialists in the industry due to ongoing and upcoming 1812 
clinical trials. This trend is expected to continue. 1813 
Variants of uncertain significance and novel genes necessitate collaborat ive efforts 1814 
to accumulate evidence of pathogenicity. This includes the establishment of a universal 1815 
reporting system and organization that can facilitate multi -center investigations. 1816 
Collaborative efforts have proven critical in investigating many ultra-rare diseases in the last 1817 
two decades. It is evident that the IRD community is inherently stronger when united, as the 1818 
conclusions drawn from multicentre studies are more valuable than a collection of case 1819 
reports and small case series. Multicentre studies of molecularly proven patients provide 1820 
greater depth of investigations and enhance the generalizability of the conclusions. While 1821 
big data analysis from electronic medical records may be helpful, they lack the granularity 1822 
of multicentre prospective studies. 1823 
Non-Mendelian inheritance may hold the key to solving a significant proportion of 1824 
previously unsolved cases. Recent examples, such as North Carolina maculopathy and 1825 
RP17 RP, highlight the importance of better understanding of the spectrum of genetic 1826 
mechanisms in IRD. Improved understanding of these mechanisms will find more 1827 
applications in the field of IRD and further accelerate advancements in human and animal 1828 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
93 
genetics, including disease modelling and novel therapeutics. Moreover, deep learning and 1829 
artificial intelligence approaches may help address the missing heritability and improve 1830 
global access to genotyping (Tan et al., 2021, Daich Varela et al., 2023f, Nguyen et al., 1831 
2023a, Veturi et al., 2023). 1832 
 1833 
7.2. Investigating the Disease Natural History 1834 
 Similar to advances in molecular genetics, advances in ultra-high resolution retinal 1835 
imaging and retinal function testing have had a dramatic multifaceted impact. These 1836 
advancements have resulted in improved knowledge of pathophysiology, pathogenesis, 1837 
disease natural history, and the relationship between genot ype-phenotype and structure -1838 
function. These aspects are fundamental to the design of clinical studies, participant 1839 
stratification, and the identification of treatment effects in clinical trials of novel therapies. 1840 
Over the last two decades, we have witness ed transformative changes in retinal 1841 
imaging, with a growing number of modalities offering greater resolution, lower cost, and 1842 
wider availability. Imaging investigations in IRD are expected to provide a greater 1843 
understanding of disease natural history and stratify patients for intervention. Biomarkers on 1844 
imaging will guide the recruitment of patients and assess their therapeutic potential. A wide 1845 
range of metrics have been explored, and there is no single metric that is ideal for all IRD. 1846 
Depending on the p henotype and genotype, more individualized studies will validate 1847 
biomarkers. Ultra-high resolution retinal imaging, with several studies already published in 1848 
the field, has provided insights into IRD at the cellular level. Over the next decade, a greater 1849 
range of imaging modalities (e.g., AOSLO, AO OCT) with faster acquisition rates, wider 1850 
fields of view, easier data management and interpretation will be available for clinical and 1851 
research practice - with increasing application of machine learning. 1852 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
94 
Beyond structural assessment for IRD, functional assessment is equally crucial. In 1853 
addition to the traditional endpoint of VA, several other endpoints have been explored, 1854 
including mobility testing, dark adaptation, microperimetry, contrast sensitivity, and FST. 1855 
Many of these tests have been validated for specific IRD, but further exploration in different 1856 
genotypes and patient cohorts is anticipated. Retrospective evaluation of baseline trial data 1857 
can indicate which groups of patients may benefit the most from an i ntervention. The field 1858 
of functional testing is evolving, and the combination of anatomical information can lead to 1859 
a better understanding of the disconnect between structure and function and identify 1860 
potential for functional rescue. The resolution of func tional testing will also increase, with 1861 
individual cell functional assessment becoming available for trials. 1862 
 1863 
 1864 
7.3. Development of Novel Therapeutics 1865 
 Meaningful progress has been made in the development of novel therapies that can 1866 
slow degeneration or improve function. However, a significant amount of further progress is 1867 
needed to address the enormous challenges faced by patients with IRD. This progress 1868 
should be based on learnings from the large number of completed or ongoing clinical studies 1869 
and trials. It is noteworthy that the first FDA-approved gene therapy for humans was for an 1870 
IRD, and the first trial of genome editing was also for an IRD. We expect the field to flourish, 1871 
and despite a number of  trials not reaching their primary endpoints in recent y ears, the 1872 
efforts will continue, and IRD will serve as a paradigm for novel therapeutics that will guide 1873 
advancements in therapeutics across the entire field of medicine. 1874 
Lessons learned emphasize the need for more detailed investigation of retinal 1875 
architecture to better predict response and stratify participants before intervention. 1876 
Additionally, it is crucial to better detect anatomical benefits after treatment and 1877 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
95 
prospectively fully characterize the natural history of IRD in large cohorts of genetically  1878 
confirmed patients. Furthermore, establishing the reliability, repeatability, validity, and 1879 
clinical meaningfulness of different structural, functional, and qualitative methods and 1880 
measurements is essential - and will be a key field in the next 5 to 10 ye ars (Daich Varela 1881 
et al., 2021, Daich Varela et al., 2022b). In larger, better-powered trials and studies, inclusion 1882 
criteria, prognostic indicators, and endpoints should be carefully and critically defined. 1883 
In the coming years, the understanding of the long -term outcomes of interventions 1884 
will improve, allowing for a more accurate risk-benefit assessment. Inflammation is a serious 1885 
concern for many novel therapeutics, and understandi ng prevention strategies and 1886 
standardizing protocols are paramount for safety and efficacy. Durability of treatment effects 1887 
is also a question, not only in terms of gene expression but also in terms of degeneration. 1888 
While RPE65 treatment has been shown to slow degeneration, it does not halt it. 1889 
Understanding the kinetics of degeneration in relation to treatment and the potential for 1890 
combination therapies, and exploration of retreatment, will be important for optimizing long-1891 
term outcomes. 1892 
Collaboration betw een academia, industry, and patient advocacy groups will be 1893 
invaluable for the successful development and translation of novel therapeutics. Academic 1894 
institutions provide the scientific expertise and infrastructure for preclinical research and 1895 
clinical trials, while industry partners bring the resources and expertise for upscaling and 1896 
commercialization. Patient advocacy groups play a vital role in raising awareness, 1897 
supporting research, and advocating for access to care/therapies. By working together, 1898 
these stakeholders can accelerate the development and availability of novel therapeutics for 1899 
IRD. 1900 
To date the majority of IRD subject to clinical trial have been recessive diseases 1901 
amenable to gene supplementation approaches. Over the next decade, autosomal dominant 1902 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
96 
diseases will be increasingly targeted by gene and pharmacological approaches. (Daich 1903 
Varela et al., 2023b) Over the same period, there will also be an increase in gene agnostic 1904 
approaches - again both pharmacological and genetic - given it is implausible that >300 1905 
gene specific therapies can be developed. We also anticipate that over the next 5 years the 1906 
first photoreceptor cell therapy trials will commence, building on the multiple RPE cell 1907 
therapy trials to date; although we predict progress will be slower than for gene therapy, 1908 
given the arguably greater current challenges that need to be overcome. 1909 
 1910 
7.4. Patient-Centred Care and Quality of Life 1911 
As advancements in molecular genetics and therapeutics continue, it is important to 1912 
prioritize patient-centred care and quality of life. While the primary goal of treatment is to 1913 
slow or halt disease progression and improve visual function, it is equally important to 1914 
consider the broader impact of IRD on patients' lives. This includes addressing the 1915 
psychosocial a nd emotional aspects of living with progressive vision loss, as well as 1916 
providing support and resources for daily living activities and mobility.  1917 
Patient-reported outcome measures (PROMs) are valuable tools for assessing the 1918 
impact of IRD on patients' qua lity of life and evaluating the effectiveness of interventions. 1919 
PROMs can capture subjective experiences, such as visual function, visual symptoms, 1920 
psychological well-being, and social functioning. Incorporating PROMs into clinical trials and 1921 
routine clinical practice can provide a more comprehensive understanding of the benefits 1922 
and limitations of different interventions. In addition to PROMs, patient engagement and 1923 
involvement in research and clinical decision-making are essential for patient-centred care. 1924 
Patients and their families should be empowered to actively participate in discussions about 1925 
treatment options, potential risks and benefits, and long -term management plans. Shared 1926 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
97 
decision-making models, where patients and healthcare providers collabora te to make 1927 
informed decisions, can improve patient satisfaction and treatment adherence. 1928 
Furthermore, access to comprehensive multidisciplinary care is crucial for optimizing 1929 
patient outcomes. This includes access to genetic counselling, low vision rehabil itation, 1930 
psychological support, and social services. Collaborative care models, where different 1931 
healthcare providers work together to address the diverse needs of patients, can improve 1932 
coordination and continuity of care. Finally, it is important to consid er the economic 1933 
implications of IRDs and the affordability of treatments. The high cost of genetic testing, 1934 
imaging, and novel therapeutics can create barriers to access for many patients. Efforts 1935 
should be made to ensure equitable access to these resources, particularly for underserved 1936 
populations. This may involve advocating for insurance coverage, government funding, and 1937 
cost-sharing programs. 1938 
 1939 
7.5. Conclusions 1940 
 In summary, this review has encompassed two decades of extensive research 1941 
dedicated to deep p henotyping a wide range of IRD  genotypes, characterising and 1942 
improving understanding of both stationary and progressive disease, isolated and 1943 
generalised retinal degeneration s, and paediatric versus adulthood -onset blinding 1944 
conditions. Our review aims  to a cquaint clinicians with the dynamic landscape of 1945 
diagnostics, imaging techniques, and therapeutic interventions in this field. Over the past 1946 
two decades, our understanding of IRD has evolved significantly. The discovery of novel 1947 
genes has enriched the gene tic spectrum of these diseases. Whilst a dvances in retinal 1948 
imaging have facilitated in depth phenotyping, broadening the clinical spectrum and enabling 1949 
a more comprehensive assessment of patients. This deeper understanding of the natural 1950 
history of IRD has improved prognostication, while developments in structural and functional 1951 
Journal Pre-proof

 
 
 
98 
evaluation have given rise to novel endpoints for clinical trials. Furthermore, the progress in 1952 
molecular biology has paved the way for the development of promising therapeutics. These 1953 
advancements have culminated in the initiation of a large number of clinical trials exploring 1954 
multiple disparate avenues, with the first novel therapeutic product now available to patients, 1955 
and numerous others in late stages of development.  1956 
In conclusion, the on -going journey in the field of IRD is marked by continuous 1957 
exploration, discovery, and innovation. As we stand on the cusp of a new era in the treatment 1958 
of IRD, it is our hope that this review serves as a valuable resource for clinicians, 1959 
researchers, and all those dedicated to improving the lives of individuals affected by these 1960 
devastating conditions.  1961
